Title: Lyman-alpha Pressure Strongly Enhances Pre-Supernova Feedback at Cosmic Dawn: The First Multi-Dimensional Lyman-alpha Radiation Hydrodynamics Simulations
Authors: Olof Nebrin, Aaron Smith, Garrelt Mellema, Kevin Lorinc, Daniele Manzoni
Published: 2026-06-01
Categories: astro-ph.GA, astro-ph.CO

Draft version June 3, 2026
Typeset using LATEXtwocolumnstyle in AASTeX631
Lyman-αPressure Strongly Enhances Pre-Supernova Feedback at Cosmic Dawn:
The First Multi-Dimensional Lyman-αRadiation Hydrodynamics Simulations
Olof Nebrin
 ,1 Aaron Smith
 ,2 Garrelt Mellema
 ,1 Kevin Lorinc
 ,2 and Daniele Manzoni
 3
1Department of Astronomy & Oskar Klein Centre for Cosmoparticle Physics,
AlbaNova, Stockholm University, SE-106 91 Stockholm, Sweden
2Department of Physics, The University of Texas at Dallas, Richardson, Texas 75080, USA
3Scuola Normale Superiore, Piazza dei Cavalieri 7, I-56126 Pisa, Italy
ABSTRACT
The dynamical role of Lyman-α(Lyα) radiation pressure feedback has been debated for nearly a
century, with recent analytical and 1D numerical studies highlighting its potential dominance over
other stellar feedback processes at Cosmic Dawn. Despite this, no multi-dimensional Lyαradiation
hydrodynamics (RHD) simulations have been performed to date. In this paper, we present the first
2D LyαRHD simulations usingLydion, an RHD code with a novel M1 moment method for Lyα
transfer, and self-consistent dust dynamics.Lydionyields a∼ O(100)×speed-up compared to Monte
Carlo radiative transfer in simple benchmarks, making 2D LyαRHD feasible. We perform simulations
of star clusters and isolated stars embedded in dense, metal-poor (Z/Z ⊙ ≤0.01) clouds, and find
that Lyαfeedback dramatically boosts outflows and dominates over feedback from direct and infrared
radiation pressure. Lyαleakage through lower-column density channels, Doppler shifts, and Lyα
photon destruction, while important, cannot prevent the build-up of strong Lyαradiation pressure in
H iiregions, leading to radiative forces∼(2−16)×L bol/c, and Lyαforce multipliersM F ∼10−60.
Lyαfeedback may not preclude efficient star formation, but raises the threshold gas surface density for
this to occur. We conclude that nearly all galaxy and star formation simulations are currently missing
the strongest source of radiation pressure feedback in dense and metal-poor environments.
1.INTRODUCTION
For nearly a century, great controversy has surrounded
the potential dynamical importance of radiation pres-
sure from multiple scatterings of Lyα(1215.67 ˚A) pho-
tons in and aroundH iiregions (for early work, see
e.g. Ambarzumian 1932; Zanstra 1934; Chandrasekhar
1945; Yada & ¯Osaki 1957; George 1973; Cox 1985;
Haehnelt 1995; Henney & Arthur 1998; O’Dell 1998;
Oh & Haiman 2002; Dijkstra & Loeb 2008; McKee
& Tan 2008). In recent years, novel analytical solu-
tions and modelling (Abe & Yajima 2018; Lao & Smith
2020; Tomaselli & Ferrara 2021; Nebrin 2022; Kapoor
et al. 2023; Nebrin et al. 2025; Smith et al. 2025), fully
coupled 1D Lyαradiation-hydrodynamic (RHD) sim-
ulations (George 1973; Smith et al. 2016, 2017b), 1D
and 3D simulations with approximate subgrid modelling
(Kimm et al. 2018; Manzoni & Ferrara 2025), and 3D
Corresponding author: Olof Nebrin
olof.nebrin@astro.su.se
post-processing results (Smith et al. 2019; Menon &
Smith 2026), suggest that Lyαfeedback can dominate
the pre-supernova feedback (‘early feedback’) momen-
tum budget in dust-poor conditions. If true, this would
have profound implications for the formation of the first
stars, galaxies, and massive black holes, now probed by
theJames Webb Space Telescope(JWST):
•Population III star formation:The very first
stars formed at high redshifts in dust-free conditions.
It has long been suspected that Lyαfeedback could
boost radiation pressure from these Population III
(Pop III) stars by∼1−3 orders of magnitude, at
least up until Lyαleakage following the breakout of
theH iiregion (e.g. Doroshkevich & Kolesnik 1976;
Oh & Haiman 2002; McKee & Tan 2008; Stacy et al.
2012; Wise et al. 2012; Jaura et al. 2022; Nebrin 2022;
Klessen & Glover 2023; Nebrin et al. 2025). This could
more easily clear surrounding dense gas, and hence
have strong implications for the Pop III star forma-
tion efficiency, the Pop III initial mass function, and
escape fraction of ionizing photons.
arXiv:2606.02711v1  [astro-ph.GA]  1 Jun 2026

2
•Formation of the first Globular Clusters and
Ultra-Faint Dwarf galaxies:The oldest globu-
lar clusters and Ultra-Faint Dwarf (UFD) galaxies
are metal-poor, and formed in dust-poor conditions
at high redshifts (Brown et al. 2014; Ricotti et al.
2016; Simon 2019; Adamo et al. 2024). Abe & Ya-
jima (2018) suggested that Lyαfeedback could be
of critical importance during the formation of metal-
poor star clusters, a conclusion which has been re-
inforced in both analytical modelling (Nebrin 2022;
Nebrin et al. 2025), and 3D simulations with approx-
imate subgrid prescriptions for Lyαfeedback (Kimm
et al. 2018). Recent observational evidence of Lyα
photon trapping around young metal-poor star clus-
ters further hints at the potential importance of Lyα
feedback (Pascale & Dai 2024; Peng et al. 2026).1 The
same conclusion likely holds for UFDs (Nebrin 2022;
Nebrin et al. 2025). Unless Lyαfeedback is properly
accounted for, this could hamper current attempts to
constrain ΛCDM using zoom-in galaxy formation sim-
ulations (e.g. Ricotti et al. 2016; Wheeler et al. 2019;
Revaz 2023; Andersson et al. 2025; Brown et al. 2025).
•Formation and growth of black holes at Cosmic
Dawn:Lyαfeedback is a crucial missing ingredient
in early black hole assembly within dense, primordial
gas reservoirs (Inayoshi et al. 2020). In direct-collapse
black hole (DCBH) scenarios, the extremely large neu-
tral hydrogen column densities (N HI ≳10 24 cm−2)
render the environment highly optically thick to Lyα
(Dijkstra et al. 2016; Ge & Wise 2017; Johnson &
Dijkstra 2017; Smith et al. 2017b). Mushano et al.
(2024) have recently shown that, at least for static
1D setups, Lyαradiation pressure can impede super-
Eddington accretion onto black holes at Cosmic Dawn.
In realistic 3D configurations, anisotropies in density
and velocity fields naturally direct where the radiation
pressure is most impactful (Smith et al. 2017a). More
broadly, the similar nature of Little Red Dots (LRDs)
invites explorations of Lyαfeedback in these contexts
too.
Despite many decades of debate about Lyαfeedback,
it has proven highly challenging, if not infeasible, to in-
corporate this process on-the-fly in simulations. This is
because conventional LyαMonte Carlo radiative trans-
1 Salgado et al. (2016) also presented observational evidence of
significant Lyαheating of dust in the Orion Nebula, although this
does not necessarily imply appreciable Lyαradiation pressure.
Baldwin et al. (1991), usingCloudy, estimated that the Lyα
radiation pressure is dynamically important in Orion, at values
of∼60% of the gas pressure. However, it would take a code with
full LyαRHD likeLydionto better test this.
fer (MCRT) codes tend to be extremely computation-
ally demanding (see discussion in Kimm et al. 2018;
Smith et al. 2018; Hopkins et al. 2020; Byrohl & Nel-
son 2025; Lorinc et al. 2025). 2 It is mainly for this rea-
son that recent studies have relied on 1D simulations
and analytical results. However, it is hard to draw ro-
bust conclusions about the importance of Lyαfeedback
in a realistic clumpy and turbulent interstellar medium
(ISM), without the aid of 2D or 3D simulations that can
capture Lyαleakage through low-column density chan-
nels (Behrens et al. 2014; Kimm et al. 2019; Kakiichi
& Gronke 2021; Yuan et al. 2024). Although recent
approximate analytical results predict that strong Lyα
feedback can survive in a turbulent ISM (Nebrin et al.
2025), this remains to be tested in self-consistent simu-
lations. Indeed, several authors have highlighted multi-
dimensional LyαRHD as a high-priority goal for un-
derstanding star and galaxy formation at Cosmic Dawn
(e.g. Smith et al. 2018; Tomaselli & Ferrara 2021; Jaura
et al. 2022; Klessen & Glover 2023; Thompson & Heck-
man 2024; Nebrin et al. 2025; Peng et al. 2026).
In this paper, we perform the first 2D LyαRHD
simulations, using a new code,Lydion(Lyman-α
Diffusion). Departing from traditional LyαMCRT
methods,Lydionemploys a novel M1 moment method
(Levermore 1984), combined with a Fokker–Planck
treatment of frequency redistribution (Rybicki 2006).
Besides on-the-fly LyαRHD,Lydionalso incorporates
comprehensive physical modelling of stellar feedback,
dust dynamics, and ISM photo-thermochemistry. In
recent years, 2D simulations have elucidated the role
of direct UV and indirect infrared (IR) radiation pres-
sure feedback in both idealized wind-tunnel simulations
(Davis et al. 2014; Zhang & Davis 2017; Rosdahl &
Teyssier 2015; Smith et al. 2020), and in simulations
of young massive (proto-)stars (e.g. Kuiper & Hosokawa
2018; Fukushima et al. 2020; Martini et al. 2026). By
including self-consistent Lyαradiation pressure for the
first time, along with several additional physical pro-
cesses often neglected in previous studies (e.g. dust dy-
namics),Lydionbuilds on this tradition, and enables
more comprehensive exploration of stellar feedback in
dense star-forming environments.
As a first exploratory study, we applyLydionto study
the importance of Lyαfeedback in metal-poor environ-
ments (0≤Z/Z ⊙ ≤0.01), relevant for the formation of
2 Smith et al. (2018) have shown that discrete diffusion MCRT
could dramatically speed up MCRT codes in the optically thick
regime relevant for Lyαfeedback. Recently, Byrohl & Nelson
(2025) have also demonstrated that GPU acceleration could ren-
der MCRT LyαRHD feasible in the near future.

old globular clusters, UFD galaxies, and Population III
stars. We highlight the results from our highest resolu-
tion (5122) simulations in Figs. 1–2, where we simulate
the feedback from a 10 4 M⊙ star cluster, embedded in
a dense (n H = 10 5 cm−3), metal/dust-poor gas cloud
(Z/Z⊙ =D/D ⊙ = 0.01, whereDis the dust-to-gas ra-
tio). In the simulation ignoring Lyαfeedback, photoion-
ization, stellar and IR radiation pressure fail to drive
outflows, leading to recollapse (and presumably more
star formation). In the simulation incorporating Lyα
radiation pressure, the cloud is destroyed. We describe
this simulation, and several others, in greater detail in
this paper, along with tests of the basic methodology of
Lydion.
The paper is structured as follows. In Sec. 2 we outline
the methodology underlyingLydion, mostly focused on
the new M1 + Fokker–Planck method for LyαRT. De-
tailed discussion and tests of the hydrodynamics code
can be found in Appendix B. In Sec. 3 we verify the M1
LyαRT method by comparing predictions against ana-
lytical LyαRT solutions and MCRT results. In Sec. 4 we
applyLydionto study feedback-driven outflows from
metal-poor star clusters and isolated stars, embedded
in dense, metal/dust-poor gas clouds. We discuss the
robustness and implications of our results in Sec. 5. Fi-
nally, we conclude and summarize our results in Sec. 6.
To aid the reader in navigating the paper, a more de-
tailed table of contents can also be found in Table 1.
A schematic overview of theLydionmethodology for
RHD simulations can be found in Fig. 3.
2.METHODOLOGY
In this section we discuss the methodology underlying
theLydioncode.Lydionis a CPU-based code, written
in theJuliaprogramming language (Bezanson et al.
2017),3 which has facilitated a combination of fast code
development and good performance.
2.1.A new M1 moment method for Lyman-α
radiative transfer
InLydion, we solve the LyαRT equation using
a new two-moment method, working in the comoving
frame. To proceed, we define the frequency-dependent
zeroth moment of the intensity (the mean intensity)
J(r, ν)≡
R
dΩI(r, ν,n)/4π, first momentH(r, ν)≡R
dΩI(r, ν,n)n/4π(related to the flux,F= 4πH),
and second momentK(r, ν)≡
R
dΩI(r, ν,n)n⊗n/4π
(related to the radiation pressure tensor,P= 4πK/c).

in the comoving frame is then governed by (Castor 2004;

The M1 moment closure, although not perfect, can
model both the diffusive optically thick regime for Lyα
transfer (D≃I/3), and beam-like Lyαescape through
low-column density channels (D≃h⊗h). The M1 clo-
sure in Eq. (4) has also been used in similar contexts to
study infrared radiation pressure feedback (Rosdahl &
Teyssier 2015; Rosdahl et al. 2015; Skinner & Ostriker
2015; Kannan et al. 2019).
The use of an M1 moment method also has the advan-
tage over flux-limited diffusion models that the spatial
transfer of Lyαphotons can be computed with an ex-
plicit rather than an implicit scheme, which will offset
some of the numerical costs of 2D LyαRT. However,
unlike the spatial transfer, the frequency diffusion and
Lyαabsorption/destruction must still be treated implic-
itly to avoid severe time-step constraints. 7 As a result,
we solve Eqs. (1)–(2) using an operator split, implicit–
explicit (IMEX) method. We discretizeJandH, as
J→J i,j,k andH→H i,j,k, where (i, j) denote the
spatial cell in cylindrical (R, Z) coordinates (assuming
axisymmetry), andkdenotes the frequency bin. For
the frequency bins, we follow Smith et al. (2018) and
use piecewise-constant bin-averaged values ofH(see Ap-
pendix A for details).

ficult to determinea prioriin an RHD simulation. An-
alytically, it is expected to be|ν−ν Lyα|≲few×
(avτ0)1/3 ∆νD, wherea v ≃4.7×10 −4 T −1/2

theH icolumn densityN HI can therefore greatly affect
the required range of the frequency grid. Furthermore,
the core of the line must be well-resolved to capture most
scatterings, as well as Lyαdestruction processes (e.g.

fixed frequency grid, similar to Mushano et al. (2024).
In particular, we use a geometrically stretched grid in
frequency-displacement spaceδν=ν−ν Lyα, symmet-
ric around line center. The two central bins have width
∆νD(Tmin), and the bin widths increase according to
∆νk+1 =q∆ν k when moving outwards into the wings,
whereq= 1.1 by default. The full frequency grid covers
|δν| ≤min[10 5 ∆νD(Tmin),0.99ν Lyα, ν LyC −ν Lyα]. We
chooseT min = 30 K by default,8 which yieldsN ν = 194
frequency bins. In Fig. 4 we show the adopted frequency
grid, along with the Voigt profile for two representative
temperatures,T= (100,10 4) K.
Our method is formally first-order accurate in time,
and second-order accurate in space and frequency. Thus,
our overall update scheme for a radiative transfer time-
step ∆tRT is as follows, in order:
1.Advection step:We first treat advection of Lyα
photons by solving:

We solve these equations explicitly for every ac-
tive frequency bin (see below), using a finite-volume
method.9 We use a dimensionally unsplit approach,
with first-order time integration. While higher-order
time integration methods exist (e.g. RK2) for M1
advection (e.g. Melon Fuksman & Mignone 2019; He
et al. 2024), they also reduce to first-order accuracy in
the optically thick regime of interest to us, while be-
ing at least twice as numerically expensive for fixed
∆tRT, and harder to combine with subcycling. For
these reasons, we have chosen to use a first-order time
integration. In contrast, second-order spatial recon-
struction has been shown to be more important for
accuracy in M1 transport (Kannan et al. 2019). To
ensure second-order accuracy in space, we linearly re-
constructJandf=H/Jat cell interfaces (see e.g.
Wibking & Krumholz 2022), and use a MinMod slope

cient cooling (below∼10 K), and the CMB background, prevents
cooling to such low temperatures.

found to give slightly better results for e.g. the emer-

By default we simulate diffuse ionizing recombination
radiation on-the-fly with M1 transport (Sec. 2.5), in
which case we adopt the Case A recombination co-
efficient from Hui & Gnedin (1997). We adopt the
Lyαemission probabilityP Lyα from Storey & Hum-
mer (1995), which is a weak function ofTandn e,
as shown in Fig. 5. For Case A recombinations and
typical conditions studied in this paper,P Lyα ∼0.4.
For continuum absorption and scattering, we consider
dust absorption and scattering (Sec. 2.5, Table 2),
Thomson scattering by electrons, inelastic Raman
scattering by H2 (Nebrin et al. 2025), and absorption
by the pseudo-molecules OHx and CHx (representing
e.g. OH and CH), which leads to their photodisso-
ciation (for details, see Appendix G.3). Besides con-
tinuum absorption, other destruction mechanisms for
Lyαphotons can become important in dense gas (Ne-

10
brin et al. 2025). The Lyαdestruction probabilityp d
is computed following Nebrin et al. (2025). In short,
we take into account 2p⇌2stransitions in collisions
withe −,H i,H ii, H 2, and Hei, as well as transitions
induced by the Cosmic Microwave Background. Also
incorporated is Lyαabsorption by neighboring lines
in H2 (Neufeld 1990; Nebrin et al. 2025).

We solve this equation separately for each cell (i, j)

A detailed discussion of the solution method is pro-

14 While there are second-order implicit methods, we have settled
on backward Euler (BDF1). The Crank–Nicholson method, while
formally second-order accurate, is only A-stable, which tends to
produce numerical oscillations. In contrast, the BDF1 method is
L-stable, and can quickly damp numerical oscillations. There is
also the second-order L-stable TR-BDF2 method (e.g. Edwards
et al. 2011), but it requires two implicit stages, hence making
the overall step twice as expensive. Furthermore, it is slightly
less damping of oscillations than BDF1, and not unconditionally
monotonic (Bonaventura & Rocca 2017). For reasons of perfor-
mance, stability, simplicity, and consistency with the convergence
order of the spatial advection method, we have opted for BDF1.
15 We note that in the optically thick limit,D→I/3, and this
approximation becomes exact.

2.2.Parallelization, and code optimization strategies
Despite improvements in performance over MCRT,
brute-force LyαM1 RT withO(200) frequency bins is,
by far, the most numerically expensive ingredient ofLy-
dion. To boost performance ofLydion, we parallelize
the M1 step over frequency bins, and the frequency dif-
fusion step over spatial cells. We use two additional
optimization techniques to speed up both the M1 ad-
vection and frequency diffusion steps:
1.Only advect active bins:Since we use a very wide
frequency grid, it turns out that many frequency bins
often contain negligibleJ, and hence have negligi-
ble impact on both the radiation pressure, and the
emergent spectra. Thus, we only advect ‘active’ bins
containing>10 −10 of the total Lyαenergy (over the
entire simulation volume). We have found that this
alone can reduce the number of bins advected by up
to a factor∼2.
2.Optimize Voigt profile calculation:The (bin-
averaged) Voigt profileHis needed for every cell and
frequency bin, and at every time-step. This quickly
becomes time-consuming as a result of several nu-
merically expensive functions involved (e.g. Daw-
son function, ande −x2
, see Smith et al. 2015, 2018),
which, if not done with care, can take up a signif-
icant portion of the overall CPU time for the Lyα
RT update. As further discussed in Appendix A,
we therefore create look-up tables for these functions
of the dimensionless frequencyx≡(ν−ν Lyα)/∆νD,
augmented by asymptotic expansions at large argu-
ments. With these look-up tables, and other related
17 We have used scalings motivated by the force multiplierM F ∼
ttrap/tlight for the dependence onp d and the continuum absorp-
tion optical depthτ c. More specifically, eqs. (68) and (77) in Ne-
brin et al. (2025), for extended sources. Physically speaking, Lyα
photons are effectively destroyed ifp dτ0 ≳1 or (aτ 0)1/3τc ≳1.
Velocity gradients will also reduce the force and trapping time,
but the effect is more modest, and analytical expressions only
exist for simplified setups (Nebrin et al. 2025; Smith et al. 2025).
For this reason, and to be conservative, we neglect velocity gra-
dients in the estimate of the required RSL.
optimizations, we have found up to∼8×speed-up of
this step compared to earlier non-optimized versions.
Future development ofLydionwill target GPU archi-
tectures, domain distributed workloads, and adaptive
mesh refinement. These developments are expected to
significantly speed up LyαRHD simulations at a given
target resolution.

2.4.Hydrodynamics, dust dynamics, and gravity
We couple Lyαand stellar feedback to a 2D hydrody-
namics and dust dynamics code. Dust dynamics is po-
tentially important because radiation pressure on grains
can, in some cases, partially drive out the dust fromH ii
regions (Akimkin et al. 2015, 2017; Ishiki et al. 2018),
and presumably lead to a stronger build-up of Lyαra-
diation pressure. The gas densityρ, momentum density
ρu, and energy densityEare obtained by solving:

binβ. To ensure accurate thermal evolution in the
high-Mach regime, we use the dual energy formalism
(see e.g. Bryan et al. 1995, 2014; Teyssier 2015), which
is implemented closely following Bryan et al. (2014).
We consider three dust bins/types: silicate grains (Sil),
graphite grains (C), and polycyclic aromatic hydrocar-

After the gravity solve + kick over ∆t hydro/2, we up-
date the hydro- and dust-dynamical conservative vari-
ables over a global step ∆t hydro,19 considering only
the advection terms, and using the partially updated
(△)–state from Eqs. (28)–(30) as initial conditions.
The resulting equations are solved using a dimension-
ally unsplit finite-volume method, with WENO3 spa-
tial reconstruction (closely following Mignone 2014), and
third-order RK3 time-integration (Mignone et al. 2007).
While WENO3 offers third-order accurate interface re-
construction in smooth regions, we do not distinguish
between fluxes at the center of an interface and the in-
terface area-averaged fluxes. As a result of this, the
code is formally second-order spatially accurate. By
default we use the HLLC flux function for hydrody-
namics, and the HLLgd flux function of Verrier et al.
(2025) for dust dynamics. For stability reasons, near
strong shocks, and regions with steep gradients, we re-
vert to second-order linear reconstruction (with a Min-
Mod slope-limiter), and the HLL flux function for the
gas. More details of the WENO3 method, and tests of
the hydrodynamics code, can be found in Appendix B.
After the advection of gas and dust, we perform an-
other gravity solve + kick over a half-step ∆t hydro/2,
to complete the full updaten→n+ 1. As described
in the next sections, other source terms (e.g. radiation
19 We also advect passive scalars, e.g. abundance fractions, during
this step.

13
pressure, drag, cooling/heating) are subcycled over (typ-
ically many smaller) substeps, the sum of which add up
to ∆t hydro. An overview of the operator-split ordering
inLydionis shown in Fig. 3.
2.5.Stellar and infrared radiative transfer, feedback &
dust modelling
To modelH iiregions and stellar feedback, we im-
plement RT in seven broad frequency bands following
Kannan et al. (2020) and Deng et al. (2024), ranging
from infrared (IR) and optical (opt), to far-UV (FUV),
Lyman-Werner (LW),H i-ionizing (EUV1), Hei-ionizing
(EUV2), and Heii-ionizing (EUV3) photons (see Table 2
for photon energy intervals):
B ∈(IR,opt,FUV,LW,EUV1,EUV2,EUV3).(31)
As for Lyα, we employ the M1 moment method to solve
forJ B andH B in each bandB, but ignoring frequency
derivative terms. Stellar emission is restricted to the
non-IR bands.Lydionallows for stellar emission from
a single star, or a star cluster. These are modelled as
follows:
•Single star:When modelling emission from a single
star, the stellar emissivity is taken to have a Gaus-
sian spatial distribution, withσ ⋆ = 3 cells by default.
Our experiments have shown thatσ ⋆ ≳2−3 is re-
quired to obtain approximately sphericalH iiregions
in uniform media (also see Skinner & Ostriker 2015;
Chan et al. 2021; Menon et al. 2023). We estimate
stellar luminosities in each band following Deng et al.
(2024), who provide SED-derived fits to photon emis-
sion rates in all bands, as a function of the zero-age
main sequence stellar massm ⋆ and stellar metallicity
10−8 ≲Z ⋆/Z⊙ ≲1. This covers the full range from
primordial Pop III stars to present-day metal-enriched
stars.
•Star clusters:When modelling emission from star
clusters, we also consider a Gaussian density profile
for simplicity, but determineσ ⋆ from the desired half-
mass radius of the star cluster,R h ≃1.538σ ⋆. Here
too we adopt photon emission rates for individual stars
from Deng et al. (2024), but IMF-average these rates
and multiply by the mass of the star cluster. We as-
sume a fully sampled Kroupa IMF (Kroupa 2001), ex-
tending from 0.08−100 M ⊙, and only consider emis-
sion/feedback from stars more massive than 8 M⊙ (see
Deng et al. 2024).
By default, we also model diffuse emission of ionizing
photons from recombinations ofH ii, Heii, and Heiii,
following Rosdahl et al. (2013) and Kannan et al. (2019),

dust model of Weingartner & Draine (2001a) as ini-
tial conditions, appropriate for denser, star-forming re-
gions (see Fig. 6). 21 More specifically, we use the data
made available by B. T. Draine for smoothed astrosili-
cate dust, graphite dust, and PAHs as a function of grain
size and wavelength. 22 The relevant dust opacities are
summarized in Table 2. We stress that, becauseLydion
incorporates dust growth and destruction (see below) as
well as dust dynamics, the total dust opacity in a given
region will evolve dynamically, beyond what is expected
from mere gas dynamics.
Absorption of stellar and Lyαphotons by dust will
lead to re-emission in the IR, and, in caseτ IR ≳1, sub-
sequent IR absorption and re-emission. The resulting
IR radiation pressure can, potentially, be important in
very dense, dusty clouds (e.g. Skinner & Ostriker 2015;
Rosdahl & Teyssier 2015; Menon et al. 2022, 2023). For
completeness, we therefore model IR transport and feed-
back. We assume that IR emission is solely by dust, such

To model growth and destruction of dust, and its cou-
pling to gas dynamics, we implement the following pro-
cesses:
•Dust growth by accretion:We allow the dust den-
sity in each bin to grow by accretion of metals in the
ISM. The rate for a dust bin/typeβ∈(Sil,C,PAH)
is proportional to the availability of the limiting gas-

dependent IR dust opacities is crucial for accurately modelling
IR radiation pressure feedback. This is naturally done inLydion,
with fully self-consistent dust physics and heating.

The accretion time-scalet gr,β,0 is estimated following
Hirashita & Aoyama (2019), adopting the recent re-
sults for the sticking coefficient on amorphous carbon
grains by Bossion et al. (2024). For accretion onto
graphite and PAH dust,ρ X =ρ C (the gas-phase car-
bon density), and for silicate dust we takeρ X =ρ Si
(the gas-phase Si density). The accretion onto silicate
dust depletes the gas-phase Si abundance, and simi-
larly for the carbon abundance with respect to growth
of graphite and PAH dust.
•Dust destruction:Dust in our simulations can
be destroyed by thermal sputtering (Micelotta et al.
2010; Choban et al. 2026), non-thermal sputtering (Hu
et al. 2019), sublimation (Guhathakurta & Draine
1989; Waxman & Draine 2000), chemical sputter-
ing of graphite grains (Lenzuni et al. 1995; Borderies
et al. 2025), and photodissociation of PAHs (Murga
et al. 2019, 2020).Thermal sputteringof silicate and
graphite grains is only important for gas tempera-
turesT≳10 5 K, which is typically only encountered
in case stellar wind feedback is simulated. However,
thermal sputtering of PAHs can be important down
toT∼10 4 K (Micelotta et al. 2010).Sublimation
of dust can be important in hyper-compactH iire-
gions (Arthur et al. 2004), where dust temperatures
can reach the threshold≳1200−1800 K for rapid
sublimation.Non-thermal sputteringcan destroy dust
moving at relative velocities≳50 km s −1 with respect
to the gas. Such high drift velocities are common near
massive stars as a result of radiation pressure on dust
(e.g. Draine 2011a; Akimkin et al. 2015, 2017; Ishiki
et al. 2018; Soliman et al. 2024).
Chemical sputteringof graphite grains can occur when
they accrete hydrogen atoms and produce hydrocar-
bons that leave the grain (Draine 1979; Bar-Nun et al.
1980; Sorrell 1992; Lenzuni et al. 1995; Borderies et al.
2025). This erosion mechanism for graphite grains
can be important in denseH iiregions (Draine 1979).
Photodissociationof small PAHs can occur following
absorption by UV photons, leading to loss of carbon
atoms in case cooling by infrared emission is ineffi-
cient (Allain et al. 1996; Murga et al. 2019, 2020,
2022; Rodr´ ıguez Montero et al. 2026). Observations
indeed show depletion of PAHs inH iiregions, con-
nected to the strong UV background in these regions
(Pavlyuchenkov et al. 2013; Egorov et al. 2023). 24
As described in detail in Appendix F.2, we estimate
the dust destruction time-scalet dest,β taking the afore-
mentioned processes into account, and add the corre-
sponding dust destruction term to the evolution of the
dust density for each dust bin/typeβ:

We compute the drag coefficientsK β taking into ac-
count normal gas-dust collisions, as well as Coulomb
drag between charged dust grains, ions, and elec-
trons (Draine & Salpeter 1979; Akimkin et al. 2015,
2017; Ishiki et al. 2018). The grain charges are com-
puted self-consistently taking into account photoelec-
tric charging by UV photons (including Lyα) (Wein-
gartner & Draine 2001b; Kimura 2016), and collisional
charging by ions and electrons (Draine & Sutin 1987).
Typically, the Coulomb drag dominates inH iire-
gions, but can still be overwhelmed by radiation pres-
sure on larger grains (Draine 2011a; Akimkin et al.
2015, 2017; Ishiki et al. 2018).
We subcycle dust growth/destruction and drag (to-
gether with radiation pressure) in the overall photo-
thermochemistry substeps (see Fig. 3). More
detailed discussions of gas-dust drag and dust
growth/destruction can be found in Appendix F.1 and
F.2, respectively.
Finally, for completeness, we have implemented stel-
lar wind feedback inLydion, following Deng et al.
24 During testing of the PAH photodissociation implementation, we
found that the simulated PAHs, withN C = 82 carbon atoms (for
the assumed characteristic PAH size), were efficiently destroyed
inH iiregions (seeq PAH in Fig. 27 for an example). This is
consistent with observations, although has been hard to explain
theoretically forN C ≳60−80 (e.g. Pavlyuchenkov et al. 2013;
Murga et al. 2019; Kirsanova & Pavlyuchenkov 2023). We are
greatly indebted to Maria S. Murga for carefully checking our
modelling assumptions and calculations. In short, we find effi-
cient PAH destruction primarily because (i) energetic He-ionizing
photons give large estimated dissociation probabilities, and (ii)
because the photoelectric emission probability estimated with the
model of Kimura (2016) is not unity at these energies, which al-
lows energetic photons to partake in photodissociation of PAHs.
Kirsanova & Pavlyuchenkov (2023) suggested that extreme UV
photons could potentially explain the destruction/depletion of
large PAHs inH iiregions. Our modelling, albeit simplified, sup-
ports their hypothesis.

(2024), although by default it is turned off to avoid se-
vere CFL time-step constraints on ∆t Hydro, caused by
hot and fast-moving shock-heated gas. Furthermore,
we note that stellar wind feedback is only important
at high metallicities. If turned on, stellar wind momen-
tum and mass is injected each global step ∆t Hydro in a
finite volume centered on the star, weighted by the local
smoothed stellar density. This injection takes place after
the final gravity update, but before subcycling of radia-
tive transfer and photo-thermochemistry (see Fig. 3).
2.6.Photo-thermochemistry
To model the thermo-chemical state of both primor-
dial and metal-enriched gas in detail, we explicitly track
the non-equilibrium photochemistry ofe −,H i,H ii, H 2,
D i,D ii, HD, Hei, Heii, Heiii,C i,C ii, CO,O i,O ii,
CHx (representing CH, CH 2, CH +, CH +
2 , CH +
3 ), and
OHx (representing OH, H 2O, OH +, H 2O+, H 3O+).25
For electrons, atomic hydrogen, and helium, we use re-
combination and collisional ionization rates summarized
in Rosdahl et al. (2013). Heating from photoionization
ofH i, H 2, Hei, and Heiifollows Kannan et al. (2020).
To better model the ISM outsideH iiregions, we also
implement photoelectric heating (Weingartner & Draine
2001b), heating from photodissociation and vibrational
excitation of H 2 (Hollenbach & McKee 1979; Baczyn-
ski et al. 2015), chemical heating (cooling) from H 2 for-
mation (collisional dissociation) (Hollenbach & McKee
1979; Yoshida et al. 2006), and heat exchange from dust-
gas collisions (see Appendix E). For photoelectric heat-
ing, because we evolve the dust (though in a coarse man-
ner), we cannot adopt fits that assume a fixed grain size
distribution. Thus, for self-consistency we instead com-
pute the heating directly, as outlined in Appendix F.3.
Heating and cooling is then subcycled in a manner
following Rosdahl et al. (2013), by solving ˙e=−Λ net
over (typically small) subcycle steps for the gas internal
energy densitye=nk BT /(γ−1), 26 where Λ net is the
netcooling rate. As in Rosdahl et al. (2013), we use a
linearized BDF1 method for stability, such that over a

We model H 2 chemistry mostly following Hopkins
et al. (2023) (for similar modelling, see Katz et al.
2017; Nickerson et al. 2018; Kannan et al. 2020; Park
et al. 2021), including H 2 formation in the gas-phase
via H −, on dust grains, in three-body reactions, and
H2 destruction by collisional dissociation (viae −,H i,
H ii, H 2), Lyman-Werner photodissociation (including
self-shielding, following Draine & Bertoldi 1996), and
photoionization by EUV1, EUV2, and EUV3 photons
(Baczynski et al. 2015; Kannan et al. 2020). The
adopted rates for the chemical reactions are mostly the
same as in Hopkins et al. (2023), with a few exceptions
where we have opted for more accurate and/or recent
fits.28 A detailed discussion of H 2 photochemistry and
cooling inLydioncan be found in Appendix G.1.
Cooling by HD can be important in primordial gas
(e.g. McGreer & Bryan 2008; Hirano et al. 2015a; Leno-
ble et al. 2024). 29 We therefore include a simple model
of deuterium chemistry, tracking the non-equilibrium
abundances ofD i,D ii, and HD, in a similar manner
to McGreer & Bryan (2008). We use updated rate coef-
ficients where possible (e.g. Faure et al. 2024), and also
add a few additional processes to those considered by
McGreer & Bryan (2008), including photodissociation
of HD, and photo- and collisional ionization ofD i(see
Table 6). We account for self-shielding by HD (Draine
& Bertoldi 1996), shielding of HD by H2 (Wolcott-Green
& Haiman 2011), and shielding of HD byH iand dust

Carbon and oxygen chemistry (C i,C ii,O i,O ii,

andC ii(157.75µm) is properly implemented by solving
for the relevant level populations, following Kim et al.

includes suppression at high column densities. Equi-

2.7.Summary of operator split RHD method
We have now described the physics implemented in
Lydion, and the corresponding methods used. To ad-
vance by a global time-step ∆tHydro, we use the operator
split order shown in Fig. 3. To summarize, hydro and
gas-dynamical advection and gravity (and stellar winds,
if on) are first considered, after which we sub-cycle radia-
tive transport, emission/absorption, and various other
stiff processes (e.g. gas cooling) within the global hydro
advection step. This ensures stability, as well as good
performance, since we avoid a hydrodynamical solve ev-
ery time we advect photons (the CFL time-step for the
latter is generally much smaller than the former). To
further ensure accuracy and stability, we limit ∆t Hydro
as follows:
∆tHydro = min(∆tCFL,∆t CFL,d,∆t grav,∆t RP),(38)
where the CFL (for gas and dust) and gravity-limited
time-steps are:
∆tCFL = CFL Hydro × 1

3.TESTING THE NEW LYMAN-αRADIATIVE
TRANSFER METHOD IN LYDION
In this section we test the accuracy of the new M1 +
Fokker–Planck LyαRT solver inLydion, by comparing
predictions to accurate MCRT results, and analytical
solutions that are valid in the optically thick regime. We
obtain the MCRT results using thecoltcode (Smith
et al. 2015). For verification tests of other code modules
inLydion— e.g. hydrodynamics, gravity, and stellar
and IR RT — we refer the reader to the Appendix.
In the tests below, as well as in our RHD simulations,
we will often study various force multipliers, which mea-
sures the boost of the Lyαradiation pressure force with
respect toL Lyα/c(e.g. Dijkstra & Loeb 2008; Smith
et al. 2019; Lao & Smith 2020; Tomaselli & Ferrara 2021;
Nebrin et al. 2025). Thus, we define:
MF ≡ 1
LLyα/c
Z
dV|f Lyα|,(43)
MF,radial ≡ 1
LLyα/c
Z
dVf Lyα ·ˆr,(44)
Among these definitions,M F is the original definition
of the force multiplier found in the literature, but does
not take the directionality of the force into account.
For this reason we also considerM F,radial, measuring
the Lyαforce directed radially outwards from the cen-
ter of the cloud (or the stellar source in RHD simula-
tions). IfM F ≫1 orM F,radial ≫1, Lyαfeedback is ex-
pected to be strong. In spherically symmetric settings,
MF =M F,radial.
33 We note that because we inject radiation pressure momentum
afterhydro advection, our scheme is always stable, since the
next CFL-limited time-step will automatically self-adjust. The
reason we useC RP <1 is rather to ensure accuracy.
3.1.Lyman-αsource in a uniform spherical cloud
As a first set of tests, we consider spherically sym-
metric and uniformH iclouds of temperatureT, and
line center optical depthτ cl =αR cl, surrounded by a
vacuum. For all these tests, the simulation box ex-
tends to 1.1×R cl in each direction, with the cloud
center placed at the origin (i.e. we assume midplane
symmetry). Outflow boundary conditions are assumed
at the outer edges of the simulation box, and reflec-
tive (i.e. zero flux) boundary conditions at the cylin-
drical axisR= 0. We set ˜c=c, and evolve up until
tmax = 4 max[ (avτcl)1/3,3]R cl/c, corresponding to at
least a few trapping times in the dust-free, static cloud
limit (Smith et al. 2018; McClellan et al. 2022). We
perform the following tests: 34
1.Static dust-free clouds (Test 1):First we con-
sider dust-free static clouds (no velocity gradients),
and further ignore Lyαdestruction, and atomic recoil.
We first consider clouds of temperatureT= 10 4 K,
with uniform Lyαemission, and Lyαoptical depth
spanning 11 orders of magnitude, 0.1≤τ cl ≤10 10.
This range covers both the optically thin and highly
optically thick regimes. In Fig. 7 we plot the Lyα
force multiplierM F predicted byLydion, for spatial
resolutions 32 2 −256 2. Also shown are the results
from MCRT tests for the same setup, which we ob-
tained withcoltusing 10 6 photons, and without any
core-skipping approximation. Since the MCRT sim-
ulations become expensive to run at highτ cl without
core-skipping, we only run these simulations up to
τcl = 108.
Finally, to testLydionpredictions at higherτ cl,
we also plot the analytical solution (Lao & Smith
2020; Tomaselli & Ferrara 2021). Analytical solu-
tions forM F are now known to be highly accurate for
τcl ≫10 3/av ≃2×10 6 T 1/2
4 , where the intensity is
nearly isotropic (justifying the Eddington approxima-
tion), and the Fokker–Planck approximation becomes
increasingly accurate (for tests, see Lorinc et al. 2025;
Nebrin et al. 2025; Smith et al. 2025). For very opti-
cally thin clouds (τ cl ≲1), we find thatLydionis in
good agreement with MCRT, with both approaching
the exact limit:

Finally, in Fig. 8 we plot the emergent spectra from
the clouds with the highest optical depth (τ cl =
108,10 9,10 10), and in Fig. 9 we plot the spheri-

2.Impact of velocity gradients (Test 2):Next we
test the ability ofLydionto capture velocity gra-
dients (Doppler shifts). In Fig. 10 we plot emer-
gent comoving spectra, from clouds expanding radi-
ally in a Hubble-like manneru= ( ˙Rcl/Rcl)rˆr, with
˙Rcl = (10,200) km s−1. We compare to the MCRT re-
sults fromcolt, obtained for the same setup in Smith
et al. (2025). The results are in good overall agree-
ment. Minor deviations are visible for 200 km s −1,
likely caused by several factors, including theτcell ∼1
transition between the GLF and diffusion fluxes in
the M1 treatment, differences in where and how the
spectra are taken (e.g.Jvs.H, or at the simulation
box edges vs. the cloud edge), chosen frames (comov-
ing vs. lab frame), the Fokker–Planck approximation,
and frequency binning. To test the latter, we run the
sameLydionsimulations with finer frequency bins
(q= 1.03, givingN ν = 594 bins), and find better
agreement with MCRT for ˙Rcl = 200 km s−1.
More important forLydion, in Fig. 11 we plot
the suppression of the Lyαforce multiplier with in-
creasing cloud expansion rate, and compare against
the analytical solution of Nebrin et al. (2025) and
Smith et al. (2025), and MCRT results without core-
skipping fromcolt. The predictions ofLydionagree
with MCRT to within<5% for ˙Rcl ≤300 km s−1. At
the highest considered cloud expansion rate, ˙Rcl ≤
1000 km s−1, we find significantly larger deviation, of
∼35%. This discrepancy is reduced to∼15% if we
increase the number of frequency bins, withq= 1.03.
The remaining discrepancy may be due to the neglect
of higher-order moment terms in the equation forH
(Eq. 2), which Buchler (1983) and Castor (2004) ne-
glect. Nebrin et al. (2025) argue that these extra
terms are small in LyαRT for expansion velocities
≲few×100 km s −1, consistent with our numerical
results.
These terms could be implemented in future ver-
sions ofLydion, following M1 treatments of neu-
trino transfer (e.g. Just et al. 2015). For now, we

3.Impact of source distribution (Test 3):So far we
have focused on clouds with uniform Lyαemission, to
avoid resolution effects for spatial point-like sources.
Nebrin et al. (2025) generalized earlier analytical Lyα
RT solutions for uniform and point sources (Dijkstra
et al. 2006; Lao & Smith 2020; Tomaselli & Ferrara
2021), to study sources of finite extent 0< r s ≤R cl.
In Fig. 12 we compare the predictions for the Lyα
force multiplierM F for dust-free static clouds with
τcl = 108 andT= 10 4 K, but varying source radius
0.05≤r s/Rcl ≤1. All simulations are run at a res-
olution of 128 2, and we ignore atomic recoil for an
“apples-to-apples” comparison. We find good agree-
ment betweenLydionand the analytical solution.

broadly agree, withLydionshowing better agree-
ment with MCRT at higher dust absorption optical
depths. As dust absorption becomes important, there
are fewer Lyαscatterings and hence a less isotropic
intensity. Since the analytical solution assumes the
Eddington approximation, this will render it less ac-
curate. In contrast, the M1 method ofLydioncan
naturally capture this regime, yielding better agree-

3.2.Anisotropic Lyman-αescape (Test 6)
Next, we test the ability ofLydionto handle
anisotropic Lyαescape. To do so, we consider an oth-
erwise spherically symmetric uniform cloud as before,
except that we introduce a bipolar lower opacity region
aligned with theZ-axis. This geometry provides a con-
trolled comparison with MCRT in a configuration where
photons can either diffuse through the optically thick
cloud or preferentially escape through lower opacity po-
lar channels (τcone ≪τ cl).
The cloud is axisymmetric about theZ-axis and is
specified in cylindrical coordinates (R, Z), with spher-
ical radiusr= (R 2 +Z 2)1/2 and angular coordinate
µ=|Z|/r. The low-opacity region is symmetric above
and below the midplane. We take the fiducial half-
opening angle of the polar channel to beθ cone =π/4
(45◦), corresponding toµ cone = cosθ cone = 1/
√
2. We
take theH idensity within the cloud (spherical radius

with ∆µ= 0.025 controlling the angular width of the
transition between the lower opacity polar region and
the high-opacity equatorial cloud. We further takeϵ= 1
for 0.5R cl < r≤R cl (theH ishell), andϵ= 10 −4 for
r≤0.5R cl (the emission region). We take the Lyα
emission to be uniform in the emission region. For sim-
plicity, the gas has a fixed temperatureT= 10 4 K,
and we choosen cl andn cone such thatτ cl = 10 8, and
τcone = 105. Atomic recoil is turned on, but we ignore
Lyαcontinuum absorption and destruction. TheLy-
diontest is performed at a spatial resolution of 256 2
cells. For comparison, we also run the same test with
colt, with different levels of constant core-skipping,
xcrit = (0,1,2,3), wherex crit = 0 corresponds to no
core-skipping approximation. To check MCRT conver-
gence, we also vary the photon count from 4×10 5 to
107. To make the cases as similar as possible, we di-
rectly feed theLydiondensity and emissivity grids into
coltto isolate the anisotropic transport effects.
In Fig. 15 we plot the predicted Lyαenergy density
fromLydionandcolt(for 10 7 photons), as well as the
H idensity for reference. Despite the strong anisotropy
in the setup, we find thatLydionis in broad agree-
ment withcolt, showing very similar anisotropic es-
cape through the cone. The energy density in the emis-
sion region is slightly higher in the MCRT run without
core-skipping than inLydion, but the factor is mod-
est,∼few×10%. In contrast, with core-skipping we
finde Lyα to be more strongly suppressed in the emis-
sion region compared toLydionand MCRT without
core-skipping.
In Fig. 16 we plot the magnitude of the Lyαradia-
tion pressure force per unit volume,f Lyα. Also shown
are the computed force multipliersM F andM F,radial.
BothLydionand MCRT predict that the force is con-
centrated at the inner edge of theH ishell. As a result,
Lydionfinds a remarkably accurate radial force multi-
plier,M F,radial = 19.37, differing by only 2% from the
MCRT results with no core-skipping (MF,radial = 19.77).
Larger errors arise for the force multiplierM F, owing to
differences in the non-radial forces that are most appar-
ent in the emission region. Here, the results ofLydion
underestimateM F by∼20% (M F = 24.45 vs. 30.76).
In contrast, the MCRT calculations with core-skipping
substantially suppress the force multiplier. Relative to
the no-core-skipping MCRT result,M F drops to 14.94,
7.754, and 6.569 forx crit = 1, 2, and 3, respectively.
Thus, core-skipping reducesM F by factors of≃2.1, 4.0,
and 4.7. We regard errors of this magnitude as unac-
ceptable in LyαRHD simulations.
Finally, in Fig. 17 we compare the emergent spectra
from the upperZ-boundary (near the cone), and near
the midplane. These spectra are not normalized to 1 to
highlight anisotropic photon escape. We find thatLy-
dionand MCRT both predict that the vast majority
of photons escape through the cone, with similar emer-
gent spectra. In summary, we conclude thatLydioncan
correctly capture anisotropic Lyαescape.

to MCRT
Finally, we quantify the computational performance
ofLydionusing the anisotropic cone test introduced
above. We first examine the scaling with spatial reso-
lution by re-running the same calculation at resolutions
322–2562, while keeping the final time and frequency

Figure 15.The Lyαenergy densitye Lyα(R, Z) andH idensity for the anisotropic cone test (Test 6).T op left panel:The

resolution fixed. For a spatial grid with resolutionN 2,
the work per timestep scales as∝N 2, while the num-
ber of radiation timesteps scales ast max/∆tRT ∝Nfor
a CFL-limited timestep. We therefore expect the to-
tal CPU cost to scale approximately asN 3. As shown
in Fig. 18, the measured timings closely follow this ex-
pectation, indicating the cost per cell update remains
approximately constant over this resolution range.
In Fig. 19 we compare the accuracy ofLydionagainst
MCRT, measuring the total (MF) and radial (MF,radial)
force multipliers against the CPU core hours. The
reference solution is taken to be the highest-photon-
count MCRT calculation without core-skipping, using
107 photon packets. This provides the cleanest avail-
able MCRT baseline because core-skipping can bias in-
ternal radiation-field quantities and derived forces. We
also include lower-photon-number MCRT runs and core-
skipping accelerated calculations with different thresh-
olds to illustrate the trade-off between Monte Carlo
noise, systematic bias, and computational cost. ForLy-
dion, we plot the CPU core hours when bothM F and
MF,radial have reached within 99% of their final time
values, since convergence to the steady state occurs well
before the maximum time evolved.
We see in Fig. 19 thatLydionis comparable in speed
to MCRT with modest core-skipping (x crit = 1), and
relatively few photons (4×10 5). Despite this,Ly-
dionis significantly more accurate than all the core-
skipping runs, which severely underestimate bothM F
andM F,radial. To achieve competitive accuracy with
MCRT, one has to discard core-skipping and use≳10 6
photons, in which case the simulation would be≳30×
more costly to run thanLydion, and still not con-
verged inM F when compared to the baseline 107 photon
MCRT calculation. Compared to the latter,Lydionis
∼300×faster to run. This benchmark shows thatLy-
dionreaches accurate force estimates at substantially
lower computational cost than direct MCRT for this op-
tically thick, dust-free problem. 36
36 MCRT without core-skipping required≃3 sec per photon packet,
while core-skipping accelerates the calculation by factors of 10.7,
762, and 2710 forx crit = 1, 2, and 3, respectively. Timings were
measured on nodes with AMD EPYC 9334 2.7 GHz processors.

Figure 19.The naive and radial force multipliersM F
(top panel) andM F,radial (bottom panel), respectively, for

This comparison should be interpreted as a represen-
tative example that may miss other aspects of these
methods. In MCRT, the cost depends on the number
of scatterings (set by the optical depth, dust absorp-
tion, destruction processes, and velocity gradients), the
core-skipping prescription, the estimator used for mo-
mentum deposition, and the target observable. In the
present test we use the scattering-based force estima-
tor, which converges more rapidly than path-based mo-
mentum estimators, and exploit the midplane symme-
try of the setup to boost statistics. For static clouds,
MCRT scales asN core
scat ∼τ 0 without core-skipping and
reduces toN wing
scat ∼(aτ 0)2/3 with core-skipping, al-
though additional optimizations are possible with dis-
crete diffusion (Smith et al. 2018, Kimura et al., in
prep.). InLydion, the cost is set primarily by the
grid size, timestep constraint (e.g., diffusion∝(aτ 0)1/3),
and number of frequency bins, and does not suffer from
Monte Carlo sampling noise. Thus,Lydionexcels in
optically thick, dust-poor systems where direct MCRT
becomes extremely expensive, but where accurate time-
dependent radiation forces are still required.
4.THE FIRST 2D LYMAN-αRHD SIMULATIONS:
METAL-POOR STAR CLUSTERS AND
ISOLATED STARS
In this section we present the results of the first Lyα
RHD simulations in multiple dimensions, and the first
ones with dust dynamics (for earlier dust-free 1D sim-
ulations, see George 1973; Smith et al. 2017b). As a
first application, we consider metal-poor star clusters
and isolated stars embedded in metal and dust-poor,
dense gas clouds. Since our simulations do not incor-
porate star formation itself, but rather a fixed stellar
source, we did not choose a realistic stellar source. In-
stead, the more basic questions we aim to answer are
the following:
1. What is the relative importance of Lyαfeedback com-
pared to other early (pre-supernova) feedback pro-
cesses in atypicaldust-poor environment?
2. Can Lyαfeedback drive outflows in such dust-poor
environments?
To address these questions, we therefore consider the
following setups:
1.Embedded metal-poor star clusters:For the
main runs with the highest resolution (in terms
of cell count), we consider low-mass (10 4 M⊙),
metal-poor (Z ⋆/Z⊙ = 0.01) star clusters, embed-
ded in a dense (n H = 105 cm−3), metal and dust-
poor cloud (Z/Z ⊙ =D/D ⊙ = 0.01) of radius
Rcl ≃0.5 pc. Outside this radius (distancer > R cl
from cloud center), the initial gas density decays as
nH ∝exp[−(r/0.05R cl)2], until reaching the ini-
tial external densityn H = 1 cm−3. The simulation
volume extends to (R max, Zmax) = (1.2,1.2) pc,
and mirror symmetry is imposed at the midplane
Z= 0.

4 km s−1, corresponding to a Mach number of
M ∼5 for gas of temperatureT∼100 K. The star
cluster has a fixed half-mass radiusR h = 0.05 pc,
giving a stellar surface density Σ ⋆ =M ⋆/2πR2

also numerically convenient, because it will more
easily create a single, morphedH iiregion around
the star cluster, as well as maximize direct and
infrared radiation pressure feedback by reducing
flux cancellation effects (Menon et al. 2023; Menon
& Smith 2026). The latter point will render our
conclusions about therelativeimportance of Lyα
feedback more conservative.
2.Embedded isolated metal-poor stars:We
also perform a series of simulations of isolated
metal-poor massive stars (35 M ⊙), embedded in
even denser gas (nH = 106 cm−3), with lower dust-
to-gas ratios ofD/D ⊙ = (0,10 −3). For the dust-
enriched scenarios (D/D ⊙ = 10 −3) we vary the
level of initial turbulenceu RMS between 0 and
8 km s−1 to check the impact on the Lyαradia-
tive force. We also run a few metal/dust-free sim-
ulations, as a preliminary assessment of Lyαfeed-
back during Pop III star formation. The luminos-
ity of the star isL bol = (1.6,2.0)×10 5 L⊙ for
Z⋆/Z⊙ = (0.01,0), with most of the starlight in
the ionizing and Lyman-Werner bands (∼70% and
∼90% for the Pop II star and Pop III star, respec-
tively).
The assumed dust-to-gas ratios in our runs deserve some
comments and context. Our setups are partly moti-
vated by the low metallicities of old and metal-poor
globular clusters (GCs), and of Ultra-Faint Dwarf galax-
ies. Typical stellar metallicities of these objects are
10−4 ≲Z/Z ⊙ ≲few×0.01, with old GCs being bi-
ased to the higher end of this range (e.g. Harris 2010;
Simon 2019; Larsen et al. 2020; Martin et al. 2022;
Fu et al. 2023; Weisz et al. 2023; Adamo et al. 2024;
Nakajima et al. 2025). Based on a naive, but com-
monly assumed, linear scaling between the dust-to-gas

(DtG) ratioDand gas metallicity, this would suggest
that these objects were born in dust-poor clouds with
10−4 ≲D/D ⊙ ≲few×0.01.
In reality, observations of local metal-poor dwarf
galaxies, and damped Lyαsystems, find that they are
significantly more dust-poor than expected from a linear
extrapolation (e.g. Fisher et al. 2014; R´ emy-Ruyer et al.
2014; Schneider et al. 2016; De Vis et al. 2019; P´ eroux
& Howk 2020; Galliano et al. 2021; Roman-Duval et al.
2022). For example, based on the observational results
of R´ emy-Ruyer et al. (2014), a galaxy of metallicity
Z/Z⊙ = 0.05 would be expected to have a DtG ratio
ofD/D ⊙ ∼3×10 −3. Indeed, I Zw 18, a local metal-
poor and star-forming dwarf galaxy, has an estimated
gas metallicityZ/Z ⊙ ∼0.03, yet an extremely low DtG
ratioD/D ⊙ ∼8×10 −4 (Schneider et al. 2016). Pas-
cale & Dai (2024) also report evidence of significant Lyα
trapping in the dense ionized gas of Godzilla, a possible
young star cluster at redshiftz= 2.37, 37 despite a gas
metallicityZ/Z ⊙ ≃0.25. As a result of this inference,
37 Whether Godzilla is a star cluster or not is debated, see Vanzella
et al. (2020) and Choe et al. (2025) for alternative hypotheses.

29
Pascale & Dai (2024) argue for a near-absence of dust
in theH iiregion where Lyαis produced. 38
Recent constraints from higher redshift (z≳6) galax-
ies are still scarce, and mostly concentrated at higher
metallicities (Z/Z ⊙ ≳0.1) (Algera et al. 2025, 2026;
Mitsuhashi et al. 2026; Takechi et al. 2026). Still, these
galaxies have inferred dust-to-metal ratios that tend to
be lower than local galaxies of the same metallicity.
Lower metallicity (Z ⋆/Z⊙ ∼0.01) high-zgalaxies have
also been detected (e.g. Hsiao et al. 2025; Nakajima et al.
2025; Morishita et al. 2025), and although they provide
no constraint on the DtG ratio, they have very low in-
ferred dust attenuation (A V ∼0−0.2).
Simulations of high-redshift galaxy formation, incor-
porating dust growth and destruction, predict similar
results, withD/D ⊙ ∼(few×0.01−0.1)×Z/Z ⊙ at high
redshifts (Choban et al. 2025; Narayanan et al. 2026).
Narayanan et al. (2026) also finds that the grain size
distribution is biased to larger grains at high redshifts,
which would further reduce dust absorption of Lyα, as
well as make dust more susceptible to evacuation by ra-
diation pressure (Akimkin et al. 2015, 2017; Ishiki et al.
2018). All of these factors would tend to boost Lyαfeed-
back. In Fig. 20 we plot the adopted initial DtG ratios
in theLydionsimulations of this paper, and how they
compare to observed low and high-redshift objects.
4.1.Lyman-αfeedback from embedded star clusters
We start by presenting the results for the simulations
of embedded star clusters. We will mainly focus on our
highest resolution run,SCLyaR512, but add results from
the lower-resolution runs to test convergence, and better
check long-term trends.
4.1.1.Qualitative results: Lyman-αdriven winds and
suppressed star formation?
The time evolution of the gas density, temperature,
Lyαenergy density, and ratio between Lyαand gas
pressures is highlighted in Figs. 1–2. In the simula-
tion without Lyαfeedback, the gas recollapses rapidly,
which would presumably trigger additional star forma-
tion in a full, more self-consistent 3D simulation. In
contrast, the introduction of Lyαpressure launches out-
flows that ultimately destroy the cloud and enhance
LyC leakage. While star formation itself is not cur-
38 To explain this, Pascale & Dai (2024) show that small dust grains
can be sublimated in the compactH iiregions. They also ar-
gue that radiation pressure can evacuate larger grains. However,
these authors neglected Coulomb drag on charged grains, which
is the dominant source of drag inH iiregions. It therefore re-
mains to be seen if dust drift is a viable explanation.Lydion
could be used to explore this in future work.

shocks,n H,shock ≃ M 2 nH ∼3×10 6 cm−3, forM ∼5,

ratio is<1 do we consider a cell a candidate site of star

Overall, we see that the simulations ignoring Lyαfeed-
back predict that nearly all remaining gas should be con-
verted into stars. This is expected in the absence of Lyα
feedback for the high total (star + gas) surface densities
encountered in our simulations (e.g. Kimm et al. 2016;
Grudi´ c et al. 2018; Fukushima & Yajima 2021; Nebrin
2022; Dekel et al. 2023; Nebrin et al. 2025; Manzoni &
Ferrara 2025). In contrast, the simulations with Lyα
feedback predict much slower star formation, only con-
verting a fraction of the remaining gas into stars. These
trends are consistent among all simulation resolutions,
although they are most evident for the lower-resolution
runs that can be evolved for longer at acceptable numer-
ical cost.39
4.1.2.Which feedback process(es) dominate?
Next we quantify the strength of Lyαfeedback, and
how it compares to other feedback forces and gravity.
In Fig. 22 we plot the evolution of the Lyαluminosity,
escape fractions of both Lyαand LyC (hν >13.6 eV)
photons, the Lyαforce multipliersM F andM F,radial
(Eqs. 43–44), and:
MF,antigrav ≡ 1
LLyα/c
Z
dVf Lyα ·(− ˆfgrav).(49)
HereM F,antigrav measures the Lyαforce in the opposite
direction of the local gravitational force. Initially, when
the cloud is spherically symmetric, we expect thatMF ≃
MF,radial ≃M F,antigrav. As non-sphericity and overden-
sities develop, we expectM F,radial andM F,antigrav to
drop belowM F. Figure 22 shows the results for the
simulationSCLyaR512, where we see that at early times
the force multiplier measures in Eqs. (43)–(44) and (49)
give nearly identical values, as expected. The values
are around∼10, indicative of significant Lyαtrapping.
As time progresses, we find thatM F slowly increases to
∼20, whereasM F,radial andM F,antigrav are somewhat
lower (∼10), and nearly identical. Aroundt∼39 kyr,
the ionization front breaks out, which causes a sharp
increase in the Lyαescape fraction tof esc,Lyα ≳50%.
However, the force multipliers only drop by a factor<2,
showing a remarkable resilience to Lyαleakage.
In Fig. 23 we compare the forces from Lyα, direct, and
infrared radiation pressure (also see Fig. 1). Direct radi-
ation pressure exerts a radially outward force of magni-
tude∼0.8L bol/cfor most of the simulation time, which
39 Our higher-resolution runs without Lyαfeedback effectively stall
as the gas collapses to high densities, shortening the time-steps
in the simulations. With Lyαfeedback, the gas is ejected, but
the simulations are costly to run at the highest resolutions.
is close to the maximum value for single-scattering ra-
diation pressure around a point-like source (L bol/c). A
temporary dip in the force occurs when a fraction of the
gas recollapses att∼57 kyr, causing the force to be
imparted on scales comparable to the star cluster, giv-
ing rise to significant flux (and hence force) cancellation
(Kim et al. 2018; Menon et al. 2023). Feedback from IR
radiation pressure is negligible,∼(10 −3 −0.01)×L bol/c,
since the dust-poor cloud is optically thin to IR photons.
In Fig. 24 we further plot the IR luminosity and radia-
tion force trapping factors,f IR,trap ≡F IR/(LIR/c). We
see that only a fraction of the stellar and Lyαluminosi-
ties are converted into IR photons. This is becauseH i,
H2, Heii, and Heiiicompete for absorption of ioniz-
ing and Lyman-Werner photons, and the relatively low
dust attenuation at lower photon energies facilitate es-
cape (except for resonantly scattered Lyα). The IR force
trapping factors are of order∼0.007 at early times, close
to what is analytically expected (f IR,trap ∼τ IR).40
Lyαpressure is seen to dominate over both direct and
IR radiation pressure in Fig. 23, exerting radial forces
∼2.5L bol/cbefore breakout of theH iiregion. The
naive force from Lyα(dashed line), i.e. including non-
radial forces, is even greater, reaching∼5L bol/c. This
highlights the dynamical importance of Lyαpressure.
In summary, including Lyαfeedback in our star cluster
simulation boosts the total radially outward radiation
pressure force by a factor∼4, and the naive force by a
factor∼7.
The next major feedback process is photoionization
(PI), which overpressurizes the gas. As seen in Fig. 2,
Lyαpressure is greater than the gas pressure through-
out most of theH iiregion at early times. This is cru-
cial for launching the outflows during the phase where
the gas is most gravitationally bound. At later times,
the gas pressure becomes increasingly important, but we
see that Lyαpressure can still exceed the gas pressure
near the ionization front, and be comparable to the gas
pressure in a significant portion of theH iiregion. As
we will discuss later (Sec. 5.1), the fact that we are
studying low-mass star clusters (10 4 M⊙) here will tend
to overemphasize the relative importance of PI feedback
during star cluster formation. For the formation of mas-
sive star clusters (∼10 5 −10 6 M⊙), we expect PI feed-
back to become increasingly unimportant in comparison

(see Eq. 45). For a typical dust temperature of∼300 K (Fig. 27),

4.1.3.Emergent Lyman-αspectra
In Fig. 26 we plot emergent Lyαspectra, before and
after breakout of the ionization front. Before break-
out, we find characteristic double-peaked spectra, bi-
ased towards the red (positive ∆v≡c∆λ/λ Lyα). This
is in line with expectations for an expandingH igas
cloud (e.g. Zheng & Miralda-Escud´ e 2002; Tasitsiomi
2006; Verhamme et al. 2006; Laursen et al. 2009; Ne-
brin et al. 2025; Smith et al. 2025). For a dust-free,
static cloud, we expect the spectra to peak at ∆v=
±η(2k BT /mH)1/2 (avτcl)1/3 withη≃0.60−0.93 (Lao
& Smith 2020), which evaluates to:
|∆v|= 837η T 1/6
100 N1/3
HI,23 km s−1 ,(52)
whereT 100 ≡T /100 K, andN HI,23 ≡N HI/1023 cm−2,
when normalized to a typicalH itemperature and col-
umn density. This is within a factor∼2 of the
wavelength peak offsets we observe before breakout,
∼400−500 km s −1 att= 30 kyr. The observed peak
separations are a factor∼1.5−2 smaller than analyti-
cally predicted for a central point source (η= 0.93), but
dust absorption and turbulence is expected to reduce the
frequency offset by a similar factor (Kimm et al. 2019;
Kakiichi & Gronke 2021; Nebrin et al. 2025). Shortly
after breakout of the ionization front in theR-direction,
we find that most Lyαphotons escape via the newly
created low-opacity channel. The emergent spectra are
narrower (peaking at|∆v| ∼100−200 km s −1), indica-
tive of fewer scatterings byH i.
4.1.4.Dust and chemical state
Lyman-αfeedback is strongly coupled to the dust and
gas chemical state, primarily via dust absorption, and
Lyαdestruction by H2 line absorption and 2p→2stran-
sitions (Neufeld 1990; Nebrin et al. 2025). In Fig. 27,
we plot the dust abundances and properties (e.g. dust
temperatures and charges), att= 15 kyr in our high-
est resolution LyαRHD simulation (SCLyaR512). We
see that the dust-to-gas ratio is somewhat lower in the
H iiregion, mostly due to depletion of graphite dust
(PAHs, although important for the dust opacity, make
up only a tiny fraction of the dust mass). PAHs are
also practically absent in theH iiregion. The deple-
tion of graphite dust and PAHs causes a noticeable re-
duction in the Lyαdust absorption opacity, by a factor
∼2−3, which further facilitates a build-up of Lyαradia-
tion pressure. The depletion of graphite dust and PAHs
is mainly due to destruction processes. In particular, the
PAHs are easily photodissociated by ionizing photons in
theH iiregion, with the higher gas temperatures also
promoting thermal sputtering of PAHs. Graphite dust
is mainly depleted by chemical sputtering, caused by the
relatively high dust temperatures (T d,C ∼few×100 K)
and gas densities (see Fig. 1) in theH iiregion (Draine
1979; Lenzuni et al. 1995).
Dust dynamics, which primarily affects the larger sil-
icate grains, has only a moderate impact on the reduc-
tion in the dust-to-gas ratio and the dust opacity. This
is because of the high grain charges in theH iiregion of

the star cluster, which causes strong Coulomb drag on
the dust. Instead, dust dynamics is mainly important in

In Fig. 28, we plot the chemical abundance ratios of
various species followed inLydion. Outside theH iire-
gion, the H2 abundance is near its initial assumed value
(xH2 ∼0.1), owing to the inefficient rate of H2 formation
in the dust-poor gas, as well as the efficient self-shielding
against the Lyman-Werner radiation from the star clus-
ter, by H 2 column densitiesN H2 ∼10 22 cm−2.41 The
large survivingH ireservoir further traps Lyαphotons
in the cloud. Inside theH iiregion itself, there is also
residualH i(x HI ∼10 −5 −10 −4) which can scatter Lyα.
We also find that most deuterium (≳80%), and a sig-
nificant fraction of carbon (∼20%), is in the form of
HD and CO, respectively. These molecules are efficient

coolants, and together withC i, 42 O i, and H2, cool the

4.1.5.Are the results converged?
In our discussions above, we have focused on our high-
est resolution run (SCLyaR512), and shown that Lyα
feedback both dominates among feedback processes, and
drives outflows for our particular setup. In contrast,
the simulation ignoring Lyαfeedback predicts collapse.
Because higher resolution simulations resolve more low-
opacity channels and gas instabilities, one might wonder

whether our results are converged. 44 To check this, in
Fig. 30 we plot the results for the Lyαforce multipliers
MF,radial andM F,antigrav, for resolutions ranging from
1282 to 5122 (the main run). Also plotted are the Lyα

dVmax(ρu·ˆr,0).(54)
Prior to breakout of the ionization front, we find barely
any difference in the force multiplier between runs, i.e.
all haveM F,radial/antigrav ≃11. The precise timing of
breakout varies between the runs, but there is no obvious
trend with resolution. For example, our 512 2 simulation
predicts breakout before the 181 2 simulation, butafter
the 362 2 simulation. Following breakout there is more
scatter in the predicted force multipliers, with precise
evolution likely tied to both the particular realization of
turbulent initial conditions, and the spatial resolution.
However, our results are nearly identical before break-
out, and qualitatively the same after breakout. The
basic prediction for our assumed setup, of strong Lyα
pressure withM F,radial/antigrav ∼10, is therefore likely
converged.

The simulations without Lyαfeedback have significantly

be evolved for long, as a result of time-step constraints
during the collapse. However, we see that there can
be delayed LyC leakage in the simulations without Lyα
feedback. We note that because we treat diffuse ioniz-
ing radiation from recombinations, this can give values
off V(xHII >0.5)∼100% even if the LyC escape frac-
tion is relatively low, since the low-density external gas
is easily ionized by diffuse photons.
4.2.Lyman-αfeedback from isolated stars, the impact
of turbulence, dust, and Lyαdestruction
Next we discuss the results for the isolated star setup,
which probe the impact of Lyαfeedback during the ear-
liest (compact) stages of theH iiregion. We vary the
level of turbulence (uRMS = 0−8 km s −1), and the dust
abundance (D/D⊙ = 0,10 −3). To check the importance
of Lyαdestruction by 2p→2stransitions and H 2 line
absorption at these high densities (n H = 106 cm−3 ini-
tially), we also run reference simulations ignoring these
processes.
In Figs. 31 and 32, we show the final snapshots of
the gas density, temperature, Lyαenergy density, and
ratio between Lyαand gas pressures. We see that in
all these simulations, Lyαfeedback is dynamically im-
portant, causing a more rapid growth of theH iire-
gion. At the final snapshot, theH iiregions are a factor
∼1.5−2×larger in the simulations with Lyαfeed-
back than in those ignoring the process. We further see
that the Lyαpressure is∼0.5−few×the gas pressure
near the outer edges of theH iiregion, with the most
dramatic effect in the dust-free simulation.
These results run counter to generally held expecta-
tions that radiation pressure feedback is negligible in the
H iiregion dynamics of isolated stars, at least for the
densities probed here (e.g. Krumholz & Matzner 2009;

Sales et al. 2014; Hollenbach et al. 2026). While this is
true for direct stellar radiation pressure feedback (and
in more dust-enriched clouds), we find that Lyαfeed-
back is∼4−16×stronger in these dust-poor con-
ditions, as we show in Fig. 33. As seen in Fig. 34,
we findM F,radial ∼15−40 for the simulations with
D/D⊙ = 10−3, andM F,radial ∼35−60 for the dust-free
simulation (with Lyαdestruction). Our simulations also
inform us about the impact of turbulence, dust, and Lyα
destruction:
•Impact of turbulence on Lyαfeedback, and
comparison to analytical predictions:As we
vary the initial turbulence fromu RMS = 0 km s−1 to
uRMS = 8 km s−1, we find that Lyαfeedback becomes

39
weaker, but only by a modest factor of∼2. This
relatively weak suppression is approximately in line
with the analytical predictions of Nebrin et al. (2025),
who generalized uniform-cloud solutions using meth-
ods of asymptotic homogenization to treat Lyαdif-
fusion in turbulent inhomogeneous media. Ignoring
dust, large-scale expansion, and Lyαdestruction and
dust absorption, Nebrin et al. (2025) find that the ra-
dial force multiplier should drop as:
MF,radial ∝[1 + (b sM)2]−4/9 ,(56)
whereb s = 1/3 for solenoidal turbulence, andMis
the Mach number. This would predict a suppression
by a factor∼3. Adding large-scale cloud expansion,
dust absorption, and Lyαdestruction yields a weaker
dependence onMthan predicted in Eq. (56), as a re-
sult of easier Lyαescape, reducing the efficiency of de-
structive processes (Nebrin et al. 2025). The net effect
can therefore be small (factor<2), consistent with
our numerical results. We leave a more detailed com-
parison against analytical predictions to future work.
•Impact of dust absorption:We find that going
fromD/D ⊙ = 0.01 (our star cluster simulations) to
D/D⊙ = 10−3 boostsM F,radial by a factor∼2−4, for
fixedu RMS = 4 km s−1. Thus, Lyαfeedback is sensi-
tive to even small abundances of dust, although the
scaling appears to be sublinear with respect toD/D ⊙.
Finally, removing dust increasesM F,radial further by
a factor of∼2 compared toD/D ⊙ = 10−3.
•Impact of Lyαdestruction:We find Lyαdestruc-
tion (pd >0) to be important for the densities probed
in the isolated star simulations (nH ∼10 6 −107 cm−3).
This is very evident in Fig. 34, where we see that for
dust-free conditions, taking Lyαdestruction into ac-
count reducesM F,radial from∼100−300 to∼35−60.
It is therefore crucial to account for Lyαdestruction
by 2p→2stransitions and H 2 line absorption in fu-
ture simulations, as also shown analytically in Nebrin
et al. (2025). We stress that this not only includes
previously considered electron and proton-mediated
2p→2stransitions (e.g. McKee & Tan 2008), but
also transitions induced by collisions with neutral par-
ticles likeH i, which can easily dominate in the cold
gas (Nebrin et al. 2025).
5.DISCUSSION
5.1.How robust are our conclusions?
We have found that Lyαfeedback is an important, of-
ten dominant, pre-supernova feedback in dust-poor con-
ditions, most prevalent at Cosmic Dawn. This conclu-
sion is consistent with analytical calculations in more
idealized settings (Abe & Yajima 2018; Tomaselli & Fer-
rara 2021; Nebrin 2022; Nebrin et al. 2025; Smith et al.
2025; Stace et al. 2025; Manzoni & Ferrara 2025), as well
as 1D LyαMCRT results for dust-free settings (Dijkstra
& Loeb 2008; Smith et al. 2017b), and 3D MCRT post-
processing results (Smith et al. 2019; Menon & Smith
2026). It is still prudent to assess how robust our con-
clusions are to any assumptions and modelling choices.
We discuss a few caveats here, and their possible impact
on our results:
•Dust modelling and assumptions:Lyαfeedback
has long been suspected to be sensitive to dust ab-
sorption. Although we find strong and dominant Lyα
feedback in the dust-poor regimes of interest here, we
still find that dust physics must be treated with care in
any realistic modelling of Lyαfeedback. To improve
realism,Lydionimplements both self-consistent dust
dynamics, and dust growth and destruction. These
factors reduce the overall dust opacity to Lyαin the
H iiregion, which promotes build-up of Lyαpres-
sure. While these processes are expected to be less
important at extremely low initial dust-to-gas ratios
approaching dust-free conditions, they are likely cru-
cial for an accurate assessment of Lyαfeedback in
clouds with higher dust abundances relevant for the
formation of metal-poor (yet enriched) star clusters
and dwarf galaxies. We urge the community to keep
this in mind in future studies of Lyαfeedback.
Greater uncertainty in our modelling is connected to
the assumed initial grain size distributions, and the
neglect of other processes. On the latter point, Hoang
et al. (2019) have argued that larger dust grains can
be spun up by radiative torques to the point of break-
ing, fragmenting into smaller grains (a process known
as radiative torque disruption, RATD; for a review,
see Hoang 2020). Since smaller grains have higher
tensile strengths and are less susceptible to disrup-
tion, this could boost the small-grain population, and
thereby boost absorption of LyαinH iiregions. How-
ever, more recent observations and modelling suggest
that RATD is unlikely to be very important in the
regimes of interest to us here. Salgado et al. (2016)
find evidence of large dust grains (≳0.1−1µm), and
significant Lyαheating of dust, in the Orion Nebula,
both contrary to naive expectations from RATD. Sim-
ilarly, Silsbee et al. (2025) show from the observed
population of larger grains in the Solar system that
RATD must be more inefficient than early predictions
suggested. This has been reinforced in more detailed
modelling of RATD by Hoang (2025), who find that
RATD can be inefficient in compactH iiregions. In

generate with the assumed initial dust-to-gas ratio.
