Figure 34.The evolution of the force multipliers (as de-
fined in Eqs. 43–49), for the isolated star simulations.T op
panel:The results for the simulations with initial DtG ra-
tioD/D ⊙ = 10 −3 are shown, with varying levels of initial
turbulence.Bottom panel:The results for the dust-free
simulations (StarLyaNoDustandStarLyaNoDest) with fixed
initial level of turbulence (u RMS = 4 km s−1), including Lyα
destruction (black lines), and ignoring it (pink lines). Lyα
destruction is seen to be very important at the high densi-
ties of these simulations, reducing the force multiplier from
MF,radial ∼100−300 toM F,radial ∼35−60. However, despite
efficient photon destruction, Lyαfeedback remains strong.
likely a significant overestimate for the gas metallicity
Z/Z⊙ = 0.01. A more realistic star cluster simulation
setup would likely haveD/D ⊙ ∼0.1Z/Z ⊙ (Fig. 20),
which would cancel the increased dust absorption from
a hypothetical dust population governed by smaller
grains. Large grains, however, are the general expec-
tations for the very dense environments studied here
(nH ≳10 5 −10 6 cm−3). Dust coagulation, not sim-
ulated in the current version ofLydion, will tend
to produce large grains in dense clouds (e.g. Ormel
et al. 2009; Silsbee et al. 2020), similar to our as-
sumed initial dust size distribution. Depletion of small
grains is also predicted at high redshifts, further bi-
asing the dust size distribution towards larger grains
(Narayanan et al. 2026). Taken together with the low
dust abundances in our simulations, we do not expect
further improved dust modelling (e.g. dust coagula-

43
tion with more dust bins) to change the basic conclu-
sions of this paper.
•Lyman-αemission:InLydionwe have, as is stan-
dard practice, considered Lyαemission from recom-
binations, and collisional excitation from the ground
state ofH i. Raiter et al. (2010) have found that in
metal-poor and dense gas, the Lyαluminosity can
be boosted significantly (for further discussions, see
Dijkstra 2014). This is due to collisional mixing of
the 2pand 2slevels (Guzm´ an et al. 2017; Nebrin
et al. 2025), and higher fractions ofH iatoms in the
n= 2 state in hotter metal-poorH iiregions. The
former effect is already taken into account inLydion,
by using the density-dependent Lyαemission proba-
bilities from Storey & Hummer (1995) (see Fig. 5).
The latter effect could boost photoionizations by pho-
tons of lower energy, thereby increasing the rate of
recombination-produced Lyαphotons.
Judging from fig. 10 in Raiter et al. (2010), these ef-
fects could boost the Lyαluminosity by a factor∼2
for Pop II stars and star clusters, or more (∼3−4) for
a top-heavy IMF dominated by hotter stars. Since we
already take one of the effects into account (the den-
sity dependence), and focus on Pop II star clusters
and not overly massive stars (35 M ⊙), it is unlikely
that we haveunderestimatedthe Lyαradiative forces
(from this effect alone) by more than a factor∼2. 45
•Use of the M1 approximation, and how it com-
pares to Monte Carlo RT:Lydionis based on
a two-moment method for Lyα, stellar, and IR ra-
diative transfer, using the approximate M1 closure of
Levermore (1984). Although the M1 model is widely
used in star and galaxy formation simulations (e.g.
Rosdahl et al. 2013; Rosdahl & Teyssier 2015; Kan-
nan et al. 2019; Hopkins et al. 2020; Chan et al.
2021; Fukushima & Yajima 2021; Grudi´ c et al. 2021;
Wibking & Krumholz 2022; Deng et al. 2024; Kimura
et al. 2025; Chan et al. 2026), one may ask whether
this induces significant errors in the calculations of
Lyαradiation pressure forces when compared to more
accurate (but slower) MCRT calculations. We argue
that the answer isnofor the following reasons. First,
we have directly verifiedLydionpredictions against
MCRT results, showing excellent agreement. Second,
radiative transfer with M1 is only expected to show
modest inaccuracies in regions withτ∼1. However,
the optical depth at line center to Lyαphotons is
45 All else being equal, this affects only the luminosityLLyα, not the
force multiplierM F, and therefore changes the forceM FLLyα/c.
typically very large (e.g.τ 0 ∼10 8 −10 11 in dense,
cold regions). Even inH iiregions the optical depth
is large (τ 0 ∼10 4 −10 5), and the emission uniform
enough, to render the Lyαintensity nearly isotropic
and well-described by M1. As pointed out by Kimm
et al. (2018), this distinguishes Lyαfeedback from
IR radiation pressure feedback, which straddles more
closely to the regime where M1 can induce modest er-
rors (τIR ∼1). Furthermore, in those regimes where
M1 becomes somewhat inaccurate, it tends tounder-
estimatethe radiation pressure forces compared to
more accurate calculations with MCRT (see Fig. 19),
variable-Eddington tensor, and ray-tracing methods
(Davis et al. 2014; Rosdahl & Teyssier 2015; Tsang &
Milosavljevi´ c 2015; Zhang & Davis 2017; Smith et al.
2020). Thus, if any noticeable inaccuracies were to
survive in our M1 treatment (despite the good agree-
ment with MCRT in testing), it is most likely that
we have modestly underestimated the strength of Lyα
feedback, particularly when ionization fronts begin to
break out and create optically thin sightlines. This
would only serve to reinforce our basic conclusions re-
garding the importance of Lyαfeedback.
•Neglect of stellar wind feedback:AlthoughLy-
dioncan simulate stellar wind feedback, we have
neglected it for performance reasons in this paper,
since even a single cell with fast, shock-heated gas
can shrink the allowed CFL time-steps by a factor
∼(10 8 K/104 K)1/2 = 100. 46 In local star-forming
clouds, stellar wind feedback can play an important
role in the overall pre-supernova feedback. Turbu-
lent mixing and conduction is expected to produce
momentum-driven winds, that can interact in com-
plicated ways with radiative feedback inH iiregions
(e.g. Lancaster et al. 2021; Geen et al. 2023; Lancaster
et al. 2025b,a; Rodriguez et al. 2026). The correspond-
ing momentum input is comparable to direct radia-
tion pressure (∼L bol/c) for stars of Solar metallicity
(e.g. Grudi´ c et al. 2018). At lower stellar metallicities,
metal line-driven winds becomes ineffective, with mo-
mentum injection dropping as ˙pwind ∝Z 0.99
⋆ (Hopkins
et al. 2023). Thus, at the metallicities considered in
this paper, we expect stellar winds to impart forces of
order≲0.01L bol/c. This is far weaker than Lyαra-
diation pressure in our simulations,∼(2−16)L bol/c
(Figs. 23 & 33). The neglect of stellar wind feedback
is therefore justified in the metal-poor regime studied
in this paper. However, in future studies of Lyαfeed-
46 Local time-stepping, with or without adaptive mesh refinement,
could alleviate this problem (Gnedin et al. 2018).

44
back in more metal-enriched clouds, it would be ideal
to include stellar wind feedback. This is not only for
its direct feedback role, but also because of the asso-
ciated dust destruction in hot gas (e.g. Kirsanova &
Pavlyuchenkov 2023).
•Mass of the star cluster, and the role of pho-
toionization feedback:In our star cluster simula-
tions, we have fixed the mass of the cluster to 104 M⊙,
corresponding to a low-mass cluster. The gas mass
is also only∼1900 M ⊙ (Fig. 21). These choices are
mostly for numerical reasons. In particular, since we
are dealing with 2D simulations, and not simulating
star formation itself, we cannot model the 3D growth
of isolatedH iiregions that later merge into a single
largeH iiregion, nor the increase in feedback from ad-
ditional star formation. To capture the latter merged
phase of theH iiregion, we are therefore forced to
choose initial conditions where the Str¨ omgren sphere
is≳the size of the cluster, which in turn puts some
constraints on the assumed gas density, cluster size
and mass. Thus, while the density of gas in our
setup is reasonable, the cluster mass is lower than
that of typical massive globular cluster progenitors,
∼10 5 −10 6 M⊙ (e.g. Vanzella et al. 2023; Adamo
et al. 2024; Vanzella et al. 2026). This in turn means
that we are likely underestimating the relative impor-
tance of Lyαpressure compared to photoionization
(PI) feedback. It is expected that radiation pressure
feedback becomes increasingly dominant compared to
PI feedback around massive star clusters and/or on
small scales (Krumholz & Matzner 2009). Observa-
tions show this to be the case for direct and IR ra-
diation pressure in compactH iiregions (e.g. Barnes
et al. 2021; Olivier et al. 2021).
To see this for Lyαfeedback, one can compare the ra-
dially directed Lyαradiation pressure forceF Lyα =
MF,radialLLyα/cto the gas pressure forceF gas ≃

Thus, in the early stages of our star cluster simulation,
we expect Lyαpressure to be greater than or compa-
rable to the gas pressure, which is indeed seen in our
simulations (Fig. 2). At later times, when theH iire-
gion has grown larger, we expect PI feedback to even-
tually dominate (also seen in Fig. 2). However, if we
consider a more typical massive and dense star cluster
at Cosmic Dawn (e.g. Kimm et al. 2016; Ricotti et al.
2016; Vanzella et al. 2023; Adamo et al. 2024; Vanzella
et al. 2026), withM ⋆ = 10 6 M⊙ andR II = 1 pc, we
expect thatF Lyα/Fgas ≃5.4 (M F,radial/10), and Lyα
feedback would clearly dominate over PI, as well as di-
rect and IR radiation pressure, and stellar wind feed-
back (as discussed earlier). Thus, for the force multi-
pliers observed in our RHD simulations (M F,radial ∼
10−40 for the simulations withD/D ⊙ = 10−3 −0.01),
Lyαfeedback is predicted to be the dominant pre-
supernova feedback process during the formation of
metal-poor star clusters at Cosmic Dawn.
•1D vs. 2D vs. 3D:Our simulations are two-
dimensional, which raises the question of how strongly
the results depend on dimensionality. At early times,
the radiation field, ionization structure, and gas dy-
namics remain close to spherically symmetric, imply-
ing that 1D simulations should be sufficient for broad
parameter exploration in this regime. The 2D sim-
ulations are nevertheless useful because they capture
non-radial motions, gas instabilities, and the timing of
asymmetric breakout. We find that the diffusive and
nearly isotropic nature of Lyαradiative transfer tends
to evenly distribute perturbations across theH iire-
gion boundary, preventing instabilities from growing
47 We have usedk rec,B ≃2.56×10 −13 T −0.83
II,4 cm3 s−1 (Draine
2011b), evaluated atT II ∼2×10 4 K.

45
freely. Once breakout occurs, however, the mor-
phology can become substantially asymmetric, even
though the global force multiplier is only reduced be-
low∼10 when the cloud is already nearly disrupted.
Fully 3D calculations may introduce additional effects
absent in axisymmetry, including porous escape chan-
nels, azimuthal fragmentation, pockets of accelerated
gas, and fallback or recycling of dense disrupted ma-
terial. However, recent 3D MCRT post-processing
results by Menon & Smith (2026) show that strong
Lyαradiation pressure survives in more complicated
3D star-forming environments, with force multipliers
broadly consistent with those found in our simulations
(also see Smith et al. 2019). Further 2D/3D LyαRHD
studies are therefore needed to quantify how multidi-
mensional phenomena affect the late-time morphology
and coupling efficiency of Lyαfeedback.
Overall, we conclude that our result that Lyαfeedback
is dynamically important in dust-poor gas is likely to be
robust. Where there are caveats (e.g. Lyαemission in
metal-poor gas, RT methodology, and dust modelling),
it is more likely that we have underestimated Lyαfeed-
back, rendering our conclusions relatively conservative.
5.2.Broader implications and future work
Our results entail an urgent need for more comprehen-
sive LyαRHD simulations and methods to accurately
interpret observations of early galaxies and star clusters
fromJWST, and to understand star formation during
Cosmic Dawn. If the forces found here persist across
a broader range of cloud properties, then Lyαpressure
may alter the disruption of natal clouds, the stellar ini-
tial mass function, the escape of ionizing radiation, the
clustering of early star formation, and the conditions
required for efficient formation of bound stellar systems
(e.g. McKee & Tan 2008; Abe & Yajima 2018; Kimm
et al. 2018; Nebrin 2022; Nebrin et al. 2025).
Several lines of research are especially promising.
First, broader parameter surveys are needed to deter-
mine how Lyαfeedback depends on cloud mass, surface
density, metallicity, dust abundance, ionizing luminos-
ity, and stellar population properties. Such studies can
establish when Lyαfeedback is dominant, or when it
may be suppressed by dust absorption, photon destruc-
tion, or rapid leakage through low-column-density chan-
nels. Second, Lyαfeedback should be explored around
Pop III stars, accreting black holes, and compact metal-
poor star clusters, where hard spectra, high gas den-
sities, and low dust opacities may all modify the Lyα
luminosity and coupling efficiency. Third, the results of
detailed LyαRHD calculations should be distilled into
subgrid models for galaxy formation simulations (Kimm
et al. 2018; Nebrin et al. 2025), where the process is
currently almost always omitted. In Table 4 we pro-
vide a summary of pre-supernova feedback implemen-
tations in contemporary high-resolution simulations of
galaxy and star formation in dust/metal-poor environ-
ments. Among these state-of-the-art simulations, the
vast majority neglect Lyαfeedback, either citing high
computational demands and/or uncertainties (Hopkins
et al. 2020; Grudi´ c et al. 2021; Jaura et al. 2022; Sharda
& Menon 2025; Meziani et al. 2026), or not mentioning
the process at all, despite its potential dominance (most
studies).48
A subset of simulations inramsesincludes a sub-
grid prescription of Lyαfeedback developed by Kimm
et al. (2018). In these simulations, Lyαfeedback is pre-
dicted to be important, reducing both the efficiency of
star cluster formation and the overall star formation ef-
ficiency (Kimm et al. 2018; Katz et al. 2026). This rep-
resented an important first step toward exploring Lyα
feedback in galaxy simulations. However, as discussed
in Nebrin et al. (2025), the adopted normalization dif-
fers from the (MCRT-verified) analytical solution by a
factor of∼3, and the model does not account for impor-
tant destruction mechanisms (e.g. 2p→2s), or photon
leakage along low-column-density channels. A more gen-
eral and accurate analytical solution for the force mul-
tiplierM F was derived in Nebrin et al. (2025), verified
with MCRT. This improved prescription could enable
more accurate subgrid treatments of Lyαfeedback in
simulations, taking into account velocity gradients, dust
absorption, turbulence, and Lyαdestruction. However,
a detailed calibration against realistic simulations with
Lydionand MCRT, and implementation in 3D simula-
tions, is beyond the scope of this paper.
The methodology introduced here also provides a path
toward more practical general-purpose LyαRHD. InLy-
dion, future improvements could include adaptive mesh
refinement, GPU acceleration, more detailed dust mod-
elling such as coagulation and grain-size evolution, and
protostellar evolution and feedback to follow the earliest
stages of compactH iiregion formation. More broadly,
the combination of moment-based spatial transport with
Fokker-Planck frequency redistribution may inform effi-
cient LyαRT algorithms for on-the-fly use in 3D simu-
lations (e.g. Smith et al. 2018; Byrohl & Nelson 2025).
48 Early discussions of feedback-free starbursts did not take Lyα
feedback into account either (Dekel et al. 2023), despite the focus
on the environments where it is expected to matter the most. Lyα
feedback is expected to introduce complications for the concept
of feedback-free starbursts at high redshifts (Nebrin 2022; Nebrin
et al. 2025; Ferrara et al. 2025; Manzoni & Ferrara 2025).

The long-term goal is to move Lyαfeedback from an ide-
alized, specialized calculation into the standard toolkit
of star and galaxy formation simulations.
6.SUMMARY AND CONCLUSION
Despite nearly a century of vigorous debate on its po-
tential essential importance, Lyman-αradiation pres-
sure has not been studied self-consistently in any multi-
dimensional RHD simulation to date. In this paper we
have presented the first 2D LyαRHD simulations, utiliz-
ing a new code,Lydion, which incorporates a novel, and
relatively computationally cheap, M1 + Fokker-Planck
treatment of Lyαradiative transfer (Levermore 1984;
Rybicki 2006). We also implement detailed treatments
of dust dynamics, dust growth and destruction, photo-
chemistry and cooling, and stellar feedback from pho-
toionization, direct and IR radiation pressure.
In our first application ofLydion, we study feedback
from metal-poor star clusters and isolated stars, embed-
ded in dust-poor clouds, relevant for the earliest galax-
ies, stars, and star clusters at Cosmic Dawn. Our results
can be summarized as follows:
1.Lyman-αpressure strongly boosts pre-
supernova feedback in dust-poor environ-
ments:For metal-poor clouds (Z/Z ⊙ = 0.01),

47
with realistic initial dust-to-gas ratios (D/D ⊙ =
10−3 −0.01), we find that Lyαpressure domi-
nates the overall radiation pressure feedback, ex-
erting a force∼(2−10)L bol/c, compared to
∼(0.6−0.9)L bol/cand<0.01L bol/cfor direct
(stellar) and infrared radiation pressure, respec-
tively (Figs. 23 & 33). In our simulations of low-
mass star clusters (104 M⊙), the Lyαpressure also
exceeds the gas pressure in theH iiregion at early
times, while being comparable at later times near
the ionization front (Fig. 2). For more massive star
clusters (105 −10 6 M⊙), we expect Lyαfeedback
to become more dominant relative to photoion-
ization feedback (Eq. 58), in which case it would
be the strongest pre-supernova feedback process
at play in these environments. Our simulation of
an isolated Pop III star reinforces the same conclu-
sion, predicting radial Lyαforces∼(10−16)L bol/c
and force multipliersM F,radial ∼35−60. We find
that the Lyαforce is concentrated near the ioniza-
tion front, where the gas becomes strongly super-
Eddington (fEdd ≳10−100).
2.Lyman-αpressure is likely to be a key reg-
ulator of the star formation efficiency at
Cosmic Dawn:By strongly boosting the over-
all pre-supernova feedback, it is likely that Lyα
feedback is one of the key processes that regulate
the star formation efficiency in dust-poor, star-
forming clouds. In our set of star cluster simu-
lations, we find that including Lyαfeedback re-
verses gas collapse and the accumulation of dense
gas, likely reducing the star formation efficiency
(Fig. 21). We stress that this does not preclude
efficient star formation in general – the added mo-
mentum input merely raises the required gas sur-
face density where star formation becomes efficient
(e.g. Abe & Yajima 2018; Grudi´ c et al. 2018; Ne-
brin 2022; Nebrin et al. 2025; Manzoni & Ferrara
2025). Indeed, earlier analytical models of star
cluster formation at Cosmic Dawn that incorpo-
rated approximate prescriptions for Lyαfeedback
predicted the formation of dense, bound star clus-
ters in very dense clouds (Abe & Yajima 2018;
Nebrin 2022; Nebrin et al. 2025), albeit less fre-
quently than runs ignoring Lyαfeedback. Detailed
studies of the impact of Lyαfeedback on the star
formation effiency will have to await simulations
incorporating star formation itself, coupled to Lyα
RHD.
3.Lyman-αfeedback is an essential missing in-
gredient in current star and galaxy forma-
tion simulations:Despite its importance, and
the growing focus on high-redshift galaxy forma-
tion in the age of theJWST, almost all simulations
neglect Lyαfeedback (Table 4), either citing the
high computational demands of LyαRHD, or not
mentioning the process at all. The results from
Lydionin this paper, together with earlier an-
alytical work (Tomaselli & Ferrara 2021; Nebrin
et al. 2025), 1D simulations (Smith et al. 2017b),
and 3D post-processing results (Smith et al. 2019;
Menon & Smith 2026), show that Lyαfeedback
cannot be ignored in any realistic simulation of
star formation in dust-poor environments.
4.Realistic dust physics cannot be ignored:
The sensitivity of Lyαfeedback to the abundance
and absorption properties of dust renders it inter-
twined with dust physics (e.g. Henney & Arthur
1998; Menon & Smith 2026). Thus, for realistic
simulations and benchmarks of Lyαfeedback be-
yond Pop III applications, it is essential to incor-
porate dust growth and destruction, as well as dust
dynamics, as done inLydion. We find that their
effect is to lower dust opacities in theH iiregion
(Fig. 27), thereby further facilitating build-up of
Lyαpressure.
5.Realistic atomic physics cannot be ignored,
which constrains LyαRHD methodology:
We find that Lyαdestruction, mainly facilitated
by 2p→2stransitions, cannot be ignored in
dense, dust-free clouds. In our simulations of
isolated massive Pop III stars in metal/dust-free
clouds of initial densityn H = 10 6 cm−3, we find
that the radially directed Lyαforce multiplier
MF,radial ≡F Lyα,radial/(LLyα/c) drops from∼
100−300 to∼35−60, when taking Lyαdestruc-
tion into account (Fig. 34). This result is driven
by processes like H(2p) + H(1s)→H(2s) + H(1s),
identified in Nebrin et al. (2025), but neglected in
earlier works.
This result puts constraints on LyαRHD method-
ology, since it requires capturing the numerous
scatterings in the line core, usually neglected with
core-skipping algorithms in traditional MCRT
methods for performance reasons (for discussions
on the impact of core-skipping on Lyαfeedback
and Lyαdestruction, see Nebrin et al. 2025; Lorinc
et al. 2025). The M1 + Fokker–Planck methodol-
ogy ofLydionnaturally captures these core scat-
terings, as do proposed rDDMC methods for Lyα
RT (Smith et al. 2018, Kimura et al., in prep.).

48
We stress that, despite the importance of 2p→2s
transitions, Lyαpressure remains∼(10−16)×
stronger than direct radiation pressure in our dust-
free simulation, while also dominating over the gas
pressure in theH iiregion (Fig. 32). We antic-
ipate that Lyαfeedback is stronger (weaker) in
less (more) dense environments, where 2p→2s
transitions are less (more) important, but leave a
thorough exploration to future work.
Taken together, these results show that Lyαradiation
pressure is not a small correction to early stellar feed-
back. In dense, dust-poor environments, it can be the
dominant pre-supernova force, substantially altering the
dynamics of the gas surrounding young stars and star
clusters. The strength of this coupling depends on dust
absorption, Lyαleakage, velocity gradients, photon de-
struction, and the geometry of the surrounding gas, but
none of these effects eliminate the build-up of strong Lyα
pressure in the regimes studied here (largely confirming
analytic predictions by Nebrin et al. 2025).
The broader implication is that nearly all current star
and galaxy formation simulations omit a feedback chan-
nel that may be central to the regulation of metal-poor
star formation at Cosmic Dawn (Table 4). This omis-
sion is especially relevant in theJWSTera, where ob-
servations are beginning to probe precisely the compact,
low-metallicity systems in which Lyαfeedback should be
most effective. Incorporating Lyαfeedback into future
simulations—whether through direct LyαRHD, acceler-
ated transport methods, or calibrated subgrid models—
is therefore essential for a complete theory of early star
formation, ionizing photon escape, and the formation of
the first galaxies and stellar systems.

create look-up tables for these functions ofP, which we have found to greatly speed up the calculation of the leakage
coefficients in Eqs. (A11)–(A13). It is straightforward to show that in the absence of velocity gradients and recoil (i.e.
P →0), the leakage coefficients above gives a central difference discretization, consistent with Smith et al. (2018). In
the opposite limit of large velocity gradients or strong recoil (i.e.|P| ≫1), we recover an upwind scheme. We solve
the tridiagonal equations (Eq. A10) usingLAPACK.gtsv!, available through the standardLinearAlgebra.jlpackage
ofJulia.

B.HYDRODYNAMICS: METHODOLOGY AND TESTS
In this Appendix, we give a brief overview of the hydrodynamics code employed inLydion, as well as showing a
series of validation tests.
B.1.Methodology
We adopt WENO3 to achieve higher-order spatial accuracy for the hydrodynamics in smooth flows. We closely follow
Mignone (2014) in implementing WENO3 in both cylindrical and Cartesian coordinates, and so in the interest of space
we leave out mathematical details here and refer to Mignone (2014). A directionally unsplit method is employed, with
third-order RK3 time-integration (Mignone et al. 2007). The geometric source term for the momentum equation in
cylindrical coordinates,P/R, is discretized using a third-order accurate method described in Mignone (2014).
To better ensure stability (e.g. avoid unphysical densities and pressures), we fall back to lower-order methods near
strong shocks, or otherwise detected problematic regions, similar to Verma et al. (2019). More specifically, we define
the dimensionless shock indicator:
∆Pi,j ≡ |Pi+1,j −P i−1,j|+|P i,j+1 −P i,j−1|
min[Pi,j, Pi−1,j, Pi+1,j, Pi,j−1, Pi,j+1] .(B20)
If 1≤∆P i,j <4, we flag the cell (i, j) as having a weak shock, and we revert to using the HLL flux function instead
of HLLC, but still employ WENO3. The use of HLL here is to minimize the risk of the carbuncle instability, which
can occur for shocks along grid lines when HLLC is employed. If ∆P i,j ≥4, we flag the cell (i, j) as having a strong
shock, and we revert to using second-order piecewise linear reconstruction (PLM) instead of WENO3 near this shock,
with a MinMod slope limiter as well as the HLL instead of the HLLC flux. We have found that WENO3 can still give
unstable results near strong density gradients. Because of this, we also revert to PLM + HLL near strong gradients
in the gas density and/or dust bin densities, defining ∆ρand ∆ρ d,β analogous to Eq. (B20), with fallback in case
max(∆ρ,∆ρ d,β)>100.
To approach third-order spatial accuracy, one has to distinguish between cell-averaged and cell-center values of
conservative and primitive variables. We convert between the two following Mignone (2014). Near sharp gradients,
this can sometimes lead to unstable or unphysical values. In those cases, we revert to the common second-order
approximation that cell-center and cell-averaged variables are equal. More specifically, we use an approach inspired by
Balsara (2012). To illustrate this, let us consider the conversion from cell-averaged to cell-central values of conservative
variablesρ,ρu R,ρu Z, andE, in reconstruction along theR-axis. If we have a candidate cell-center density (ρ c)i,j, we
correct it as follows:
(ρc)i,j =τ(ρ c)i,j + (1−τ)ρ i,j ,(B21)
where 0≤τ≤1 is a correction factor, andρ i,j the cell-averaged density. For smooth flows,τis typically unity,
and there is no deviation from the third-order prediction. If there are strong gradients however, the correction factor
should approach 0. We determineτsuch that (ρ c)i,j has values in the range:
0.6 min(ρi,j, ρi+1,j, ρi−1,j)<(ρ c)i,j <1.4 max(ρ i,j, ρi+1,j, ρi−1,j).(B22)
This allows for smooth extrema, as long as they are within a reasonable range. Corresponding bounds are taken for
the other conservative variables (taking into acocunt the sign for momenta), and then we chooseτto be theminimum

value computed, and derive the corresponding cell-central values of all conservative variables using thisτ. Furthermore,
we also revert toτ= 1 if the corresponding pressure or density is still unphysical, or if the cell has been flagged as
containing a shock. Finally, in the interface reconstruction, we only accept a candidate WENO3 reconstruction if the
interface values are physical, and do not differ too much from cell-average values (Titarev & Toro 2004). If a candidate
WENO3 interface reconstruction is rejected, we revert to PLM, and if that still fails, we revert to a simple first-order
piecewise-constant reconstruction. Besides the fallback methods, we note that our methodology is at most second-order
spatially accurate since we do not distinguish between interface-center fluxes and interface-averaged fluxes.
B.2.Tests of the hydrodynamics code
Here we present a selection of standard tests of the hydrodynamics module inLydion.

B.2.1.Sedov-Taylor blast wave
To test the ability ofLydionto handle strong shocks and to maintain reasonable symmetry, we set up a spherical
Sedov-Taylor blast wave test in cylindrical coordinates (R, Z). A uniform, initially static, medium of ambient density
ρ0 = 1 and ambient pressureP 0 = 10−10 is set up. The size of the simulation volume is 0≤R≤1.5 and 0≤Z≤1.5,
with symmetry acrossZ= 0 (i.e. only upper half of the blast is simulated). The adiabatic index is fixed toγ= 5/3,
and we impose reflective boundary conditions on all boundaries. Within a central, approximately spherical, region
of radius∼5 cells, we initiate a blast of energyE blast = 1 by raising the pressure in this region accordingly. The
simulation is evolved up tot= 0.4. Snapshots from two simulations with resolution 128 2 and 512 2 are shown in
Fig. 35. For both runs, we find a spherically symmetric blast wave, with a shock radius in good agreement with the
analytical solution. In Fig. 36 we plot the spherically averaged density and pressure profiles att= 0.4 from the 512 2
simulation, and compare to the analytical predictions from the Sedov-Taylor solution. We find excellent agreement
with the analytical solution, showing thatLydioncan correctly handle strong shocks.
B.2.2.Sod shock tube
Next we set up a sod shock tube test, to check the ability of the code to capture both shocks and contact disconti-
nuities. A uniform 2D Cartesian grid (X, Y) is set up, with resolution ranging from 128×32 to 2048×32, and box
size 0≤X≤1 and 0≤Y≤1. Initial conditions areρ(X≤0.5) =P(X≤0.5) = 1 andρ(X >0.5) = 0.125,
P(X >0.5) = 0.1, withu X =u Y = 0 everywhere, and an adiabatic indexγ= 7/5. The results att= 0.2 along the
X-axis atY≃0.5 are plotted in Fig. 37, along with the exact analytical solution. Overall we find good agreement
with the analytical solution, with better convergence at higher resolution.

B.2.4.Kelvin-Helmholtz instability
Finally, a Kelvin-Helmholtz test is set up in 2D Cartesian coordinates, with resolution 512 2. The initial conditions
follow those described in McNally et al. (2012), and the simulation was evolved up tot= 5.0. The adaibatic index is
set toγ= 5/3. Periodic boundary conditions are adopted at the left and rightX-boundaries, and reflective boundary
conditions on the top and bottomY-boundaries. Four snapshots from the test are shown in Fig. 39, showcasing the
expected development of the Kelvin-Helmholtz instability.
C.SELF-GRAVITY
C.1.Solving the Poisson equation and computing the acceleration
We aim to solve the Poisson equation∇ 2Φ = 4πGρ tot efficiently in 2D. We have found an SOR iterative method
(Teyssier 2002) to be too slow, and for simplicity we have avoided implementing a faster but more complicated multigrid
50 The setup for the test follows https://www.astro.princeton.edu/
∼jstone/Athena/tests/rt/rt.html.
51 Consistent with the findings of Fleischmann et al. (2019), we
have found that the test result is subject to symmetry breaking
if the initial conditions are not perfectly symmetric, or if other
floating point errors are not properly handled. We have there-
fore carefully set up the test to maintain symmetry by mirroring
initial conditions (rather than evaluating cos), and also followed
Fleischmann et al. (2019) in the use of bracketing in the HLLC
flux to minimize these errors.

direction implicitly at a time. For a detailed description of the method applied to the Poisson equation, we refer the
reader to Black & Bodenheimer (1975), Norman & Winkler (1986), and Stone & Norman (1992). In this method, we
aim to solve the following related parabolic equation, for each cell (i, j):

We choose the pseudo-time steps following Black & Bodenheimer (1975) and Norman & Winkler (1986), and iterate
until maxi,j(|∇2Φ−4πGρ tot|/4πGρtot)<10 −6. Furthermore, we follow Stone & Norman (1992) and only update Φ
in case the density has changed significantly enough to render the current Φ inaccurate (see their eqs. 86–87, but
we adopt a tolerance 10 −6 instead of 10 −5). This procedure can significantly lower the time spent on the gravity
solver (also see Grudi´ c 2021). To compute the gravitational accelerationg=−∇Φ, we cell-averagegin a manner
consistent with the discretization of the Poisson equation (Eqs. C25–C26). Thus, to obtain (g R)i,j, we make the linear
approximation within a cell (i, j):
gR(R, Z)≃g i−1/2,j + (gi+1/2,j −g i−1/2,j) R−R i−1/2
∆Ri
,(C27)
where ∆Ri =R i+1/2 −R i−1/2 is the cell width. We then cell-average this profile to yield, after some simplification:
(gR)i,j =ω i−1/2gi−1/2,j +ω i+1/2gi+1/2,j , ω i−1/2 = 1−
¯Ri −R i−1/2
∆Ri
, ω i+1/2 = 1−ω i−1/2 .(C28)
One gets an analogous expression for the cell-averaged acceleration in theZ-direction, (g Z)i,j. For a uniform Cartesian
grid, or far away from the symmetry axis, one findsω i−1/2 =ω i+1/2 = 1/2, consistent with Mullen et al. (2021). To
be consistent with our discretization of the Poisson equation, we further haveg i+1/2,j =−(Φ i+1,j −Φ i,j)/( ¯Ri+1 − ¯Ri)
andg i−1/2,j =−(Φ i,j −Φ i−1,j)/( ¯Ri − ¯Ri−1).
C.2.Near pressure-free collapse test
To test both the Poisson solver and the coupling to hydro, we perform a nearly pressure-free collapse test, following
Skinner et al. (2019) (also see e.g. Stone & Norman 1992; Truelove et al. 1998; Almgren et al. 2010; Mignone 2014).
In particular, we use 2D cylindrical coordinates and a uniform grid, and we set up a uniform spherical cloud of initial
radiusR cl = 6500 km, and initial densityρ cl = 109 g cm−3. The initial gas density outside isρ= 10 −5 ρcl, and the
grid extends a distance 2R cl away from the cloud center along theRandZaxes. The adiabatic index isγ= 5/3,
and the initial gas pressure is uniform, and taken to beP 0 =ϵ(4πG/γ)ρ 2
clR2
cl, withϵ= 10 −7 to ensure negligible
pressure (Skinner et al. 2019). We perform the test both assuming mirror symmetry atZ= 0 (i.e. only upper half
of the cloud simulated), and for no imposed mirror symmetry (i.e. cloud center placed atZ=Z max/2, so the whole
cloud is simulated). The simulations are evolved up tot= 0.065 sec≃0.978t ff. The results for two resolutions,
NR = 128 andN R = 512, are shown in Fig. 40, with and without assumed mirror symmetry. We find no significant
difference whether we assume mirror symmetry or not, indicating that the solver can maintain symmetry throughout
the evolution. There is also good overall agreement with the analytical predictions, especially for the peak density. The
cloud radius becomes somewhat smeared towards the later stages of the collapse, owing to the non-zero gas pressure
and finite resolution. This is also seen in other codes (e.g. Stone & Norman 1992; Truelove et al. 1998; Mignone 2014;
Skinner et al. 2019), and we find that there is a sharper cloud boundary at higher resolutions.

D.STELLAR RADIATIVE TRANSFER: D-TYPE PHOTOIONIZATION TEST
In this Appendix, we test the ability ofLydionto capture the evolution ofH iiregions. To do this, we perform
test 5 of Iliev et al. (2009), wherein the R and D-type evolution of an ionization front (IF) in a uniform medium is
studied. As in Iliev et al. (2009), we initialize a 15 kpc×15 kpc box of resolution 128 2, densityn H = 10−3 cm−3, and
temperatureT= 100 K. The gas is pure hydrogen, i.e. we ignore helium, deuterium, metals, and dust. Radiation
pressure is also ignored in this test. We place a source at (R, Z) = (0,0), which emits 5×10 48 s−1 ionizing photons
in the interval 13.6 eV≤hν≤100 eV, with aT eff = 10 5 K black-body spectrum. Following Rosdahl et al. (2013)
and Kannan et al. (2019), who also employ the M1 moment method, we adopt a minimum reduced speed of light of
˜cmin = 0.01cfor this test, to correctly capture the fast initial R-type IF expansion. The simulations are evolved for
500 Myr. In Fig. 42 we compare the results fromLydionto those fromArepo-rt(Kannan et al. 2019), as well as the
ray-tracing code C 2–ray coupled to theCapreolehydrodynamics code (see Mellema et al. 2006; Iliev et al. 2009).
We plot and compare the evolution of the ionization front radius and velocity (left), and gas profiles at different times
(right). We find broad agreement between all codes, withArepo-rtandLydionpredictions being most similar. This
is expected, given the similar M1-based RT method, and identical frequency band division, in both codes.

F.DUST PHYSICS
F.1.Dust-gas coupling from drag and radiation pressure
As described in the main text, we treat each dust bin as a pressureless fluid that experiences forces from radiation
pressure, gravity, and gas drag (for similar modelling, see Paardekooper & Mellema 2006; Ishiki et al. 2018; Verrier
et al. 2025). Dust advection is operator split and treated the together with, and similar to, the gas dynamics. After
gas and dust advection, the relevant operator-split equations for dust and gas read:

latter. When considering RPD, we keep the densities constant during the update of dust and gas velocities, so that

As discussed below, the drag coefficient in general depends on|u rel|, giving rise to a non-linear set of equations. We
therefore use Picard iteration, going through the above steps with updated estimates ofK β each iteration. If this
converges, we accept the update.54 If not, we retry the whole subcycle step with a smaller ∆t sub. It is straightforward
to show that for strong drag (largeK β), the velocities are coupled (u ••
d,β ≃u ••), with the total (dust + gas) radiation
pressure affecting both components. In contrast, in the low-drag regime, the dust is decoupled from the gas, and could
be partially evacuated from denseH iiregions by radiation pressure on the dust (Draine 2011a; Akimkin et al. 2015,
2017; Ishiki et al. 2018).
The drag coefficient isK ≡ |⟨n grF drag⟩|/|urel|, whereF drag is the drag force on a single grain, andn gr is the grain
number density, with⟨⟩denoting an average over the grain size distribution (for a given bin). The drag force on a
single grain of sizea gr is (Draine & Salpeter 1979):

The dust growth and destruction time-scales are estimated as follows:
•Dust growth by gas accretion: We estimate the dust accretion growth time-scale as follows. A single grain of
a given type (silicate, graphite, or PAH), and grain sizea gr, accretes mass at a rate ˙m gr = 4πa 2
grvrel,XρXSX/fX,
wherev rel,X =
p
kBT /2πmX,S X is the effective sticking coefficient, and X is the rate-limiting species, making up
a fractionf X of the grain mass (Hirashita 2012). For silicate and graphite grains, Hirashita (2012) considers the
rate-limiting species to be silicon and carbon, withf X = 0.166 andf X = 1, respectively. We treat PAHs similarly
to graphite grains, with carbon being the rate-limiting species. The dust growth time-scale is then determined from
1/tgr,β ≡(1/ρ d,β)
R
dagr (∂ngr,β/∂agr) ˙mgr,β. This yields 1/t gr,β = 3vrel,XρXSX/fX¯agr,βρgr,β, where ¯agr ≡ ⟨a3
gr⟩/⟨a2
gr⟩
(see Appendix E for values). The growth time-scalet gr,0,β in the low-depletion limit (which is what enters Eq. F55)
is thent gr,0,β =f X¯agr,βρgr,β/3vrel,X(ρd,β +ρ X)SX. For the sticking coefficientS X we use a prescription based on
the computational results from Bossion et al. (2024). For the sticking onto amorphous carbon grains, Bossion et al.
(2024) compute chemisorption sticking coefficients ofS CI ∼0.1−0.4 depending on temperature, andS CO = 0.
While sticking of CO and CH can happen by physisorption, the CO and CH would be subject to thermal desorption
and photodesorption near massive stars, and hence not contribute to net grain growth in regions of interest (Bossion
et al. 2024). 59 In the absence of detailed data for ions, and to be conservative, we assume thatC iihave the same
sticking coefficient asC i. Thus, for the net growth of carbonaceous grains (graphite and PAH dust), we take the
effective sticking coefficient (or accretion efficiency) to be:
SC =S CI(T)[x CI +x CII ¯J(Z gr,C/PAH,1,¯agr,C/PAH, T)],(F59)
wherex CI/CII ≡n CI/CII/nC, and ¯Jaccounts for the Coulomb modification of the capture cross-section for ions
accreting onto charged grains (see Eq. F53 and Draine & Sutin 1987). While Bossion et al. (2024) computed
SCI assuming that the dust and gas temperatures are equal, that is generally not the case inLydion. However,
preliminary results sugges that the sticking coefficient is most sensitive to the gas temperatureT(D. Bossion, private
communication), and we therefore interpolate the data from Bossion et al. (2024) forS CI as a function ofT. We
are not aware of any recent data for the sticking coefficient of Si-species on silicate grains (although that may soon
change, see Hansson et al. 2025), and Si chemistry is not tracked in the current version ofLydion. We therefore
setS Si =S C for simplicity. We note however that the growth time-scale, for the grain size distribution assumed
here, is mainly set by accretion onto carbonaceous dust, and so we do not expect the overall dust growth rate to be
sensitive to the specific value ofS Si.
We implement the following dust destruction processes inLydion:

on dry (ice-covered) carbon nanoparticles, at 10 K. We are not
aware of measurements for sticking of CH (as a proxy for CH x),
but the thermal desorption time-scale for graphite grains can be

ice-covered grains will have higherE D (ED ≃2044 K, Sil et al.

in the first place ( ¨Oberg et al. 2009; Hollenbach et al. 2009).

•Dust destruction by sublimation: Hot grains can lose atoms by sublimation. We estimate the sublimation

andT d ≳1500 K for carbonaceous dust.
•Dust destruction by thermal sputtering: Thermal sputtering of larger grains (silicate and graphite) is
implemented using the fits of total yieldsY tot (inµm yr −1 cm3) from Choban et al. (2026) to the data of
Nozawa et al. (2006). The destruction time-scale (in yrs) for silicate and graphite dust is then estimated as
tsput,th = (¯agr/1µm)/3n HIIYtot. Thermal sputtering of silicate and graphite dust will only be important in gas
withT≳10 5 −10 6 K, usually not encountered inH iiregions inLydionunless stellar wind feedback is simulated.
Thermal sputtering of PAHs, on the other hand, can be efficient down to∼10 4 K (Micelotta et al. 2010; Rodr´ ıguez
Montero et al. 2026). The sputtering time-scale for PAHs is estimated using fits of yields from Micelotta et al.
(2010) for PAHs ofN C = 100 carbon atoms. AlthoughN C = 468 (¯agr,PAH/10−3 µm)3 ≃83 inLydion, the yields
of Micelotta et al. (2010) depend only weakly onN C overN C = 50−100. The PAH sputtering time-scale is then
estimated ast sput,th =N C/(Relectrons +R H +R He +R C), whereR i is the sputtering rate (in s −1) from speciesi.
•Dust destruction by non-thermal sputtering: Non-thermal sputtering occurs when there is a significant relative
velocity between gas and grains (≳50 km s−1), which can be marginally important in compactH iiregions near stars,
where drift velocities can be large (Draine 2011a; Akimkin et al. 2015, 2017). We implement non-thermal sputtering
using the fitted yieldsY tot from Hu et al. (2019) for silicate and carbonaceous grains, and estimate the destruction
time-scale ast sput,nth = ¯agr/3nHYtot. In absence of calculations for PAHs, we use the yields for carbonaceous
grains for PAHs too. Given that thermal sputtering is efficient down to lower temperatures than for graphite grains
(Micelotta et al. 2010), this probably underestimates the rate of non-thermal sputtering of PAHs. On the other
hand, PAHs are more easily destroyed by photodissociation, and so in practice, our choice here is unlikely to have a
big effect on PAH destruction.
•Graphite dust destruction by chemical sputtering: Several authors have noted that carbonaceous dust in
particular can be destroyed by chemical sputtering. Collisions with hydrogen atoms can produce hydrocarbons (e.g.
methane, CH4) that leave the grain, chipping away at the carbon mass of the grain. Draine (1979), while skeptical
of earlier high estimated chemical sputtering yields, still found that graphite grains in dense (n H ≳10 5 cm−3)
H iiregions could be destroyed by this process. Bar-Nun et al. (1980) provide experimental evidence for efficient
hydrocarbon formation on graphite, even at low dust temperatures. Lenzuni et al. (1995) surveyed more recent
literature and provided updated yields, which were also recently implemented by Borderies et al. (2025). We
implement the carbon yield for graphite grains by Lenzuni et al. (1995), which is a function of the graphite dust
temperature (Td,C):
Ychemsp(Td,C) = 4.9×10 −3 exp

fragmentation between then:th and (n+ 1):th IR photon emission. Thenφ n =p n+1

G.PHOTOCHEMISTRY & COOLING
G.1.Molecular Hydrogen Photochemistry & Cooling
Molecular hydrogen is an important coolant in primordial gas where the first stars form, and can also destroy Lyα
photons. Here we briefly outline the modelling of H 2 chemistry inLydion. The H 2 fractionx H2 ≡n H2 /nH, with
nH ≡n HI +n HII + 2nH2, evolve according to:
˙xH2 = [R dust,H2 +k H−,H2 ne +k 3B,H2 nHI(nHI +n H2 /8)]xHI (G82)
−(k diss,HInHI +k diss,HIInHII +k diss,H2 nH2 +k diss,ene + ΓH2
LW + ΓH2
ion)xH2 .
In this equation,R dust,H2,k H−,H2, andk 3B,H2 are the rate coefficients for H 2 formation on dust grains, in gas via H −,
and in 3-body reactions, respectively. Finally,k diss,j, ΓH2
LW, and ΓH2
ion are the rate coefficients for collisional dissociation
of H2 by speciesj, Lyman-Werner photodissociation of H 2, and photoionization of H 2, respectively.
The gas-phase formation of H 2 primarily takes place via H −, in two steps:
H +e − →H − +hν(rate coefficientk 1, fit from Galli & Palla 1998),(G83)
H + H− →H 2 +e − (rate coefficientk 2, fit from Kreckel et al. 2010).(G84)
The hydrogen anion H− can also be destroyed before producing H 2, including from photodetachment by photons with
energyhν >0.754 eV (McLaughlin et al. 2017), and in collisions withH i,H ii, ande −:
H− +hν→H +e − (rate coefficient ΓH−

We model the H 2-cooling rate Λ H2 (in erg s −1 cm−3) using the same functional form as in Galli & Palla (1998):
ΛH2(T, nH2 , nHI, nHeI, nHII, ne) = ΛH2,n→0
1 + ΛH2,n→0/ΛH2,LTE
,(G98)
where the LTE cooling rate ΛH2,LTE(T, nH2) (important atn H ≳10 4 cm−3) is taken from Hollenbach & McKee (1979).
For the low-density regime, we have several contributions:
ΛH2,n→0 = ΛH2,HI(T, nH2 , nHI) + ΛH2,HeI(T, nH2 , nHeI) + ΛH2,HII(T, nH2 , nHII) + ΛH2,e(T, nH2 , ne).(G99)
We take Λ H2,HI from Galli & Palla (1998), and the rest of the contributions from Glover & Abel (2008). Although
Glover & Abel (2008) updates the fit for Λ H2,HI over Galli & Palla (1998), later more accurate calculations have found
the fit from Galli & Palla (1998) to be slightly more accurate at relevant temperatures (see results and discussion in
Coppola et al. 2019; Flower et al. 2021; Nebrin et al. 2023).

G.3.Carbon and Oxygen Photochemistry & Cooling
In order to properly model cooling byC i,C ii, CO, andO i, we use a simplified model of carbon and oxygen
chemistry to track the non-equilibrium abundances of these species. Simplified models (compared to full PDR codes)
of CO chemistry of varying complexity and realism exist in the literature (e.g. Nelson & Langer 1997, 1999; Glover &

by evaluating eq. (4) of Lipovka et al. (2005) atn H = 106 cm−3.

1999; Draine 2011b; Gong et al. 2017; Khatri et al. 2024). CH x and OHx can also form viaC iiandO ii, respectively
(e.g. Glover & Jappsen 2007; Gong et al. 2017). Since we are mainly interested in low-metallicity environments, we
will neglect grain-assisted recombinations, and leave such additions to future work. The relevant reactions are given

The overall updaten→n+ 1 is only expected to be an accurate representation of a full BDF1 update if the
renormalization induces modest changes inx OI,x OII, andx OHx. In practice, this is controlled by the global photo-
thermochemistry subcycling: after each chemistry substep, we reject the update and retry with a smaller time-step
if selected key abundances change by more than 10%. For the C/O network, these quantities includex CO,x CII, and
xOII, except at very low metallicities or high temperatures where the C/O chemistry is dynamically unimportant. We
do not impose a 10% rule on every individual reaction channel, since the reactions can be extremely stiff (e.g. because
of the rapid charge exchange reactionsk HII,OI andk OII,HI), while the (semi-)implicit update remains stable.

(σOH,FUV, σ OH,LW) = (2.03,4.02)×10 −18 cm2 ,(G127)

76
For absorption of Lyα, we take (σ OH,Lyα, σ CH,Lyα) = (4.07,0.05)×10 −18 cm2 (see table 1 in Heays et al. 2017). We
note that the Lyαcross-sections are only accurate to within a factor of∼2 and∼10 for OH and CH, respectively
(Heays et al. 2017). We model self-shielding of OH x, CH x, and CO in a highly simplified manner, using the above
cross-sections to model absorption by these molecules. This approach is less accurate than interpolations of detailed
line-by-line calculations (e.g. Visser et al. 2009), but likely more accurate than the model of Nelson & Langer (1997)
which neglects self-shielding altogether.
For low-temperature gas, we implement cooling fromO i,C i,C ii, and CO, mainly following Kim et al. (2023)
and Deng et al. (2024). ForO i(63.18µm, 145.5µm) andC i(609.13µm, 370.41µm) line cooling, we model the
respective atoms as three-level systems, and include collisional rates fore −,H i, H 2,H ii, and Hei, as compiled in
Draine (2011b). Similarly,C ii(157.75µm) cooling is implemented, modelling the system as a two-level atom, taking
into account collisions withe −,H i, H 2, and Hei(also using rates compiled in Draine 2011b). For CO, we model the
cooling rate Λ CO (in erg s −1 cm−3) as:
ΛCO(T, nCO, nH2) = 2.16×10 −27 T 3/2nH2 nCO
1 + (nH/ncrit,CO)(1 +N CO/NCO,crit) ,(G130)
wheren crit,CO = 1.9×10 4 T 1/4 cm−3 andN CO,crit = 6.66×10 14 Tcm −2. The functional form for Λ CO is the same
as in Hollenbach & McKee (1979), but corrected (by a factor∼2) in the low-density regime to agree with the fit in
Whitworth & Jaffa (2018) (who have the sameT 3/2 scaling).
